Subsection (c) permits declination for a novel or complex state-law issue, substantial predominance over the original-jurisdiction claims, dismissal of all claims supplying original jurisdiction, or exceptional circumstances presenting other compelling reasons. Discretion to decline cannot supply absent statutory power.

- Finley v. United States required statutory authorization for the additional-party jurisdiction it rejected; section 1367 supplies that authorization within its terms and supersedes the contrary result at that scope.
- Zahn v. International Paper Co. no longer requires every class member to satisfy the amount requirement when section 1367 covers the related claim.
- Clark v. Paul Gray, Inc. no longer requires a separate sufficient amount for each joined plaintiff whose claim falls within the supplemental grant.
- City of Chicago v. International College of Surgeons is applied for the separate inquiries into original jurisdiction, related-claim authority and discretionary retention.
- Grupo Dataflux v. Atlas Global Group retains its citizenship rule; an insufficient amount on a related claim is a distinct statutory issue.
- Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis retains its rule concerning an actual party dismissal that cures the citizenship defect before trial and judgment.

Federal courts must identify an independently sufficient original claim, verify citizenship and same-case relatedness, apply subsection (b)'s actual exclusions and then consider any proper subsection (c) ground for declining jurisdiction. An otherwise covered class member or Rule 20 plaintiff may litigate a smaller related claim under that sequence. The pre-enactment separate-amount rules yield only to the extent the later grant reaches the claim.

### 2005-06-23 — [Gonzalez v. Crosby, No. 04-6432; 545 U.S. 524.](../records/gonzalez-v-crosby_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

##### I. A genuine federal-process motion receives its own Rule 60 assessment

**Holding and operative rule:** In a section 2254 state-custody proceeding, a Rule 60 motion confined to an asserted defect in federal habeas procedure that prevented adjudication of an already pleaded claim is not a second or successive application under 28 U.S.C. §2244(b) merely because reopening could eventually permit custody relief. Courts must examine the relief actually requested: a new custody claim, new factual merits supplement or renewed adjudication of constitutional merits remains subject to the applicable successive-application restrictions, and a mixed filing must have its genuine procedural request separated from those demands.

**Authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court, delivered by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls unanimously. The same nine Justices adopt the independent Rule 60 requirements stated here.

**Controlling explanation:** Abdur'Rahman v. Bell supplies the distinction between correcting federal procedure and renewing an attack on the state conviction. A limitations ruling that prevents consideration of an already pleaded claim performs the same threshold function as the procedural barrier addressed there. Gonzalez challenges that federal ruling alone. The district court therefore may consider his motion without advance appellate authorization under §2244(b).

Florida argues that allowing the motion would circumvent the statutory restrictions because reopening could lead to custody relief. That eventual possibility does not change what the motion asks the federal court to correct. Classification turns on substance and the integrity of the federal proceeding, rather than on the use of the word claim. Calderon v. Thompson concerns reopening a completed merits determination and therefore supplies a different predicate.

Rule 60(b)(6) separately requires extraordinary circumstances and a motion made within a reasonable time. It cannot displace the distinct requirements governing the Rule's other grounds. Applicable certificate requirements also retain their independent conditions, including constitutional and procedural reasonable-debate showings where required. These safeguards preserve finality while allowing genuine federal-process correction. Classification and entitlement to relief remain separate inquiries.

##### II. The complete showing is insufficient for extraordinary relief

**Holding and operative rule:** On the complete showing presented, the intervening procedural interpretation, the absence of an earlier merits hearing, the alleged interruption of ordinary review, Gonzalez's incarceration and self-representation, and his subsequent course of pursuit do not establish extraordinary circumstances under Rule 60(b)(6). Denial is proper even assuming the alleged timely rehearing submission, returned-paper information and resulting misunderstanding of the remaining review opportunity; the decision adopts neither a fixed monthly deadline nor a categorical exclusion of intervening legal changes.

**Authority:** Part II of Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion is joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. These seven Justices supply the controlling ground for affirmance. Stevens and Souter dissent from this application and remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** Abdur'Rahman requires an independent assessment of timing, ordinary review and the asserted legal development. Artuz v. Bennett clarifies the distinction between state filing conditions and claim-relief bars. Assume for this motion that the original limitations interpretation was mistaken. That later clarification and the lost first merits opportunity are relevant, but they do not by themselves make a final judgment subject to continuing ordinary review.

The Court also assumes that Gonzalez timely attempted rehearing, received the described information with the returned paper, and consequently misunderstood the opportunity for further review. That episode concerns the earlier appellate route. It does not itself explain the separate eight-to-nine-month period between Artuz and the reopening request. Incarceration and self-representation bear on reasonable pursuit, but the complete showing supplies no sufficient connection between the asserted earlier impediment and the later course of pursuit to make this combination extraordinary. Pace v. DiGuglielmo supports assessing an asserted obstacle against the actual period of pursuit; its contextual diligence inquiry is not a numerical cutoff.

The dissent would require a discretionary remand because the categorical successive rule prevented an individualized assessment. But the Court's sufficient ground accepts the disputed account rather than resolving credibility against Gonzalez. The legal clarification, first-merits interest and asserted review episode have all been considered with the later course of pursuit. Their combined weight remains insufficient. A credibility remand therefore cannot change the result on that ground.

#### Precedent treatment

- **Abdur'Rahman v. Bell, 537 U.S. 88 (December 10, 2002):** Applied to the federal limitations barrier. Its substance-based process distinction, separation of mixed filings, independent Rule 60 requirements and separate certificate conditions remain controlling; its case-specific remand does not require remand when denial rests on a sufficient ground accepting the movant's disputed account.
- **Artuz v. Bennett, 531 U.S. 4:** Retains its distinction between application-filing requirements and claim-relief bars. The procedural clarification is considered in the extraordinary-circumstances inquiry; the original limitations interpretation is assumed mistaken for that inquiry.
- **Pace v. DiGuglielmo, 544 U.S. 408:** Retains the rule that an unsatisfied genuine filing deadline remains a filing condition despite exceptions. Its reasonable-pursuit analysis supports examining the relation between an asserted obstacle and distinct periods of delay in their actual circumstances.
- **Calderon v. Thompson, 523 U.S. 538:** Distinguished because recall reopening completed habeas merits differs from correction of a nonmerits federal barrier. Its exceptional completed-merits requirements retain their force in that setting.
- **Rhines v. Weber, 544 U.S. 269:** Distinguished as governing retention of a timely pending mixed petition. Reopening a final judgment remains subject to Rule 60's independent requirements.
- **Pliler v. Ford, 542 U.S. 225:** Its protection for proved court-caused loss retains the predicates of causation, reasonable diligence and a lawful effective remedy. The present assumed-account ruling leaves that protection intact.

#### Law after decision

The district court may consider a genuine limitations-only Rule 60 motion without successive-application authorization. Abdur'Rahman's process distinction, separation of mixed filings, independent extraordinary-circumstances and reasonable-time requirements, and applicable certificate conditions continue to govern. Courts assess the complete showing and the actual relation between an asserted interruption of review and later pursuit; a procedural legal change does not automatically reopen a final judgment, and elapsed months alone do not replace that inquiry.

### 2005-06-23 — [Green Party of New York State v. New York State Board of Elections; 389 F.3d 411 (Second Circuit).](../records/green-party-v-new-york-state-board-of-elections_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

#### I. The substantial enrollment burden lacks the required regulatory fit

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State maintains official affiliation information for political organization as well as primary administration, erasing and refusing the affiliations of organizations with demonstrated statewide ballot support imposes a substantial associational burden. Under the calibrated Anderson–Burdick inquiry, an important state objective must have a demonstrated substantial relationship to that exclusion; the preliminary findings establish a substantial likelihood that New York's categorical exclusion fails that requirement.

**Authority:** Part I receives six direct joins: Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Its substantial-burden classification and application control without combining them with the Chief Justice's severe-burden rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** Burdick requires examination of the actual burden within the electoral system. Cartwright gives independent work to a substantial burden: serious injury need not make political success impossible, but it requires a substantial connection between the restriction and an important objective. The organizations retain nomination, campaigning and other political activity. Their exclusion from a useful continuing official channel nevertheless causes concrete injury to identification, communication and organization.

Norman requires support rules to fit the electoral function at issue, and Timmons requires a real relationship between an associational restriction and the asserted stability interest. A threshold governing primary and statutory party status does not by itself justify destroying or refusing affiliation information. Accurate instructions can distinguish recorded association from primary eligibility.

The State and dissent regard enrollment as a limited, publicly financed primary service whose incidental organizing value need not be extended. That description overlooks how the existing system operates. The State supplies continuing nonprimary advantages to established parties while removing them from organizations with demonstrated statewide support. The challenged erasure contributes insufficiently to administering primaries or avoiding confusion. The same actual-burden-and-fit inquiry resolves the overlapping First and Fourteenth Amendment claims; unequal treatment does not automatically establish severe burden.

#### II. Ongoing associational loss warrants the existing interim protection

**Holding and operative rule:** The continuing loss of identification and communication with willing affiliates establishes irreparable associational injury, and the preliminary findings satisfy the substantial-likelihood requirement applied to this affirmative injunction. Effective interim protection must preserve existing affiliations and permit new affiliations for the qualifying organizations while the merits proceed.

**Authority:** Part II has seven direct joins: the six Justices joining Part I and Stone-Zsela. Agreement concerns the need and scope of interim protection, with the Chief Justice relying on his separately stated merits ground.

**Controlling explanation:** A static copy of former lists cannot identify new voters or preserve a continuing channel of political organization. The preliminary record establishes both losses. Preserving only old data would therefore leave part of the continuing injury intact. The findings meet the substantial-likelihood standard the lower court used for affirmative relief. This interlocutory determination preserves effective association during litigation; final merits proceedings remain necessary under the same governing constitutional standard.

#### III. Implementation must cure the common exclusion for the qualified category

**Holding and operative rule:** Interim relief retains the Other write-in mechanism with accurate primary-eligibility instructions, continued maintenance and updating for organizations satisfying the established support criterion, best efforts to notify already disenrolled Liberal and Right to Life voters of reenrollment, and equal-form access to Green Party enrollment data. The decree must cure the policy for organizations and voters subject to it who satisfy the support condition, unless the State shows that narrower relief fully cures the same defect; an equally effective administrative arrangement remains permissible under supervision.

**Authority:** The same seven Justices directly join Part III.

**Controlling explanation:** Clingman requires a remedy for the unlawful common policy rather than a limitation justified solely by naming particular plaintiffs. The district court must apply the objective qualification requirement and determine an administrable scope. Continuing data maintenance applies to organizations with sufficient support to place a statewide candidate on the ballot at the most recent gubernatorial election. The form may permit a voter to name any organization without requiring continuing maintenance for every name supplied.

The separate notice and data commands perform distinct work. Best-efforts notice gives already disenrolled Liberal and Right to Life voters an opportunity to reenroll using the new form. The Green Party data order bars publication of its voters as unenrolled and requires the same enrollment data in the same form supplied to statutory parties. Equivalent implementation may replace particular mechanics if it preserves those functions. Existing protection continues while implementation and scope are settled. Separate primary eligibility and full-party requirements remain operative.

- Burdick v. Takushi supplies the actual-burden inquiry within the complete electoral system.
- Cartwright v. Barnes supplies the substantial-burden requirement of an important objective and a demonstrated substantial relationship to the restriction.
- Norman v. Reed is applied to the connection between a support requirement and the particular electoral function.
- Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party is applied to require an actual connection between a restriction and claimed electoral stability.
- Clingman v. Beaver supplies the common-policy remedy rule and the State's opportunity to show a fully effective narrower cure.
- Belitskus v. Pizzingrilli reinforces examination of actual access burdens; its inability-to-pay application remains distinct.

Qualifying political organizations receive protection against the selective loss of ongoing official affiliation information under the substantial-burden standard. States retain objective support criteria and primary-eligibility rules, but must justify the different enrollment restriction by its actual function. The injunction continues while the district court resolves final merits and applies the governing qualification and common-policy remedy requirements.

### 2005-06-23 — [Halbert v. Michigan, No. 03-10198.](../records/halbert-v-michigan_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

The seven Justices joining the Court's opinion directly support all three holdings at the stated level of generality.

#### I. Counsel must assist the initial appellate merits examination

**Holding and operative rule:** The Fourteenth Amendment requires appointed advocacy for an indigent plea-convicted defendant seeking initial appellate examination of alleged error in the Michigan Court of Appeals. A State may use a genuinely equivalent advocate-assisted screening mechanism and valid frivolousness procedures, but must supply the legal-assistance function before deciding the initial application.

**Controlling explanation:** Douglas protects fair access to initial merits review. Ross permits later discretionary selection after the defendant has received counseled appellate consideration and the materials that consideration produces. Halbert has received no such first presentation. Michigan's intermediate court examines the merits of the claimed error; the leave label cannot replace attention to that function.

Smith v. Robbins requires an appellate advocate's actual legal-screening contribution while allowing equivalent formats. A plea narrows the available claims but leaves potentially difficult questions concerning sentence, plea and representation. Post-grant counsel comes after the initial screening, and an application form cannot identify and present legal issues as an advocate can. Resource conservation and the narrower range of surviving issues therefore do not justify the missing first-review function.

Michigan's statute provides meaningful but bounded exceptions. Appointment is mandatory for an indigent plea defendant when the prosecutor seeks leave, the sentence exceeds the upper limit of the minimum applicable guideline range, an appellate court grants leave, or the defendant challenges a conditional plea under Rule 6.301(C)(2) or its successor. Appointment is discretionary when all three scoring conditions hold: an alleged offense-variable or prior-record-variable scoring error, objection or other preservation, and an upward departure above the upper limit of the minimum range the defendant contends should have applied. Those routes do not supply the assistance denied here. The constitutional protection follows the initial merits function rather than those exceptions alone.

#### II. The whole reported plea record does not establish surrender of this distinct assistance

**Holding and operative rule:** A guilty or no-contest plea does not itself waive counsel for the first appellate review that remains available. The State must establish a knowing, intelligent and voluntary surrender of that distinct assistance from the actual record; Halbert's reported advice, confirmations and general plea finding do not establish that surrender.

**Controlling explanation:** Godinez distinguishes capacity from actual waiver. Tovar requires real understanding without a prescribed judicial formula. Stumpf permits the whole record, including counsel's actual explanation and the defendant's confirmation, to establish informed assent. These rules require weighing the affirmative evidence rather than invalidating a waiver because particular words are absent.

Halbert confirmed that he understood loss of an appeal as of right and the described mandatory and discretionary appointment categories. The court found his plea understandingly made, voluntary and accurate. Counsel gave the reported affirmative responses concerning other promises or considerations and compliance with the no-contest rule. Those facts support Michigan's contextual argument, but they do not identify an explanation and intentional surrender of the distinct assistance needed to prepare the remaining leave application. Stumpf's counsel representation specifically concerned the essential elements whose understanding was disputed; the general confirmations here concern different subjects. The content of any additional promise is unspecified.

The statute commands advice about the ordinary appointment consequence, a determination of understanding and provision of a nontechnical application form. That command is not evidence that every unreported act occurred or a substitute for constitutional waiver. Halbert's later requests and reported learning limitations bear on the inquiry but neither make a valid waiver revocable nor alone prove incapacity. On the combined record, the State has not established the particular informed surrender it invokes.

#### III. Restoration supplies the missing first-review function

**Holding and operative rule:** When required first-tier appellate advocacy was wholly absent and was not validly waived, the defendant must receive renewed adequate consideration with appointed assistance without first proving that an omitted argument probably would prevail. Halbert must receive counsel, relevant-record access and a meaningful renewed application period, subject to ordinary preservation and constitutionally adequate frivolousness screening.

**Controlling explanation:** Robbins restores a missing process whose value lies in finding and presenting potentially arguable issues. Requiring an uncounseled defendant to demonstrate a winning omitted claim would demand the very professional work withheld. The state court must therefore permit an assisted application before treating the first review as complete. Valid screening may conclude that no arguable issue exists; it must include the required advocate function.

- Douglas v. California is applied to initial appellate error correction and extended expressly to Michigan's first-tier leave process.
- Ross v. Moffitt is distinguished by the earlier counseled presentation and later selective function on which its ruling rests.
- Smith v. Robbins supplies the actual advocate-assisted screening requirement, equivalent-format permission and restoration remedy for a wholly absent function.
- Godinez v. Moran preserves the independent inquiry into actual knowing, intelligent and voluntary waiver.
- Iowa v. Tovar permits flexible proof of understanding without mandatory ritual warnings.
- Bradshaw v. Stumpf is applied as a whole-record rule; its specific counsel explanation is distinguished from general plea-compliance confirmations.
- Kowalski v. Tesmer retains its third-party relationship holding; Halbert presents his own denial.

Indigent plea defendants seeking initial merits review in the Michigan Court of Appeals are entitled to appointed advocacy before the leave determination, subject to valid waiver and adequate frivolousness procedures. The State must establish an actual informed surrender rather than infer it automatically from the plea. A wholly missing, unwaived function requires restored assisted review under Robbins.

### 2005-06-23 — [Kelo v. City of New London, No. 04-108.](../records/kelo-v-city-of-new-london_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

#### Actual public redevelopment, private implementation and favoritism

**Holding and operative rule:** A compensated condemnation implementing an adopted comprehensive redevelopment plan satisfies public use when it serves a rationally supported actual public purpose rather than a pretext for private favoritism, even though private parties will implement portions and the particular homes are not blighted. Courts must meaningfully examine supported claims that the stated public purpose masks a private favor, using the actual plan, process and beneficiary relationships under the ordinary presumption of lawful public action and ordinary proof rules.

**Authority:** The Court's six-Justice opinion, whose author and complete joins appear in Opinion Topology, directly controls the entire rule and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** Berman permits an integrated redevelopment undertaking and private implementation rather than requiring every parcel to be independently harmful. Midkiff makes the public objective, rather than the mechanics of the transfer, decisive. Boston & Maine applies that rule to private reconveyance while retaining an enforceable prohibition on naked private favoritism.

The adopted plan responds to prolonged economic distress through coordinated commercial, residential and public uses. That established undertaking supplies an actual public objective beyond replacing one owner with another. Individual homes need not be blighted for their inclusion in an otherwise public plan. Forecasts of jobs and revenues support the purpose without becoming guarantees; a court need not establish that every expected benefit will materialize.

The owners and common dissent object that every ordinary successor use can promise greater productivity, making all property vulnerable. The answer lies in the public character of the actual undertaking and the independently enforceable private-favor boundary. Reciting economic benefits cannot validate a sham whose actual object is favoring a private recipient and whose public gains are merely incidental. Supported allegations require genuine record examination. On this record, the established planning purpose supports the condemnations and supplies no unresolved pretext issue requiring remand.

The Constitution does not confine privately implemented public acquisitions to removal of already harmful conditions. Public ownership and enforceable public access are sufficient examples of public use under these precedents, but do not exhaust the public-purpose rule. Just compensation and stricter state restrictions retain their independent force. An isolated transfer devised for a favored recipient is outside the rule sustained here.

- Berman v. Parker is applied to integrated public redevelopment and private implementation; individual parcel blight is not an indispensable condition.
- Hawaii Housing Authority v. Midkiff is applied to the actual public objective rather than the ultimate titleholder.
- National Railroad Passenger Corp. v. Boston & Maine Corp. is applied to public purpose despite private reconveyance and its surviving prohibition on naked favoritism. Its railroad statutory need presumption does not govern this municipal plan.
- Lingle v. Chevron U.S.A. Inc. retains the distinction between regulatory-taking liability and independent constitutional restraints, including the public-use condition.

The public-purpose rule permits the challenged comprehensive redevelopment condemnations with compensation and lawful authority, while requiring meaningful examination of supported private-favor allegations. The operative inquiry concerns the actual undertaking and its public object; economic forecasts need rational support rather than guaranteed success. State law may impose stricter acquisition limits.

### 2005-06-23 — [Mayle v. Felix, No. 04-563.](../records/mayle-v-felix_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

#### Relation back requires shared operative facts, not merely the same conviction

**Holding and operative rule:** Under Rule 15(c)(2), an amended habeas ground asserted after limitations expire relates back only when it shares a common core of operative facts set forth or attempted in a timely pleading. A new legal theory, additional detail or fair elaboration of an imperfect pro se account may qualify; the same trial, conviction or requested release alone does not supply the factual connection.

**Authority:** All seven Justices joining the Court's opinion directly adopt the rule, its stated qualifications and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 2242 permits civil-rule amendment, but Rule 15(a)'s permission to amend differs from Rule 15(c)(2)'s effect on an expired limitation. The latter provision links the amendment to conduct or events attempted in the original pleading. Habeas Rule 2(c)'s requirement of supporting facts for each ground gives that link practical content because a prosecution can contain many independent alleged wrongs.

Scarborough permits completion of an already identified timely request. Tiller permits a different legal theory concerning the same identified event. Neither makes an entire criminal judgment the pleaded transaction for every later allegation. Courts must read actual factual allegations fairly, including pro se attempts, rather than demand unchanged legal labels or professional drafting.

The contrary trial-occurrence reading emphasizes that both evidentiary challenges concern one trial and that Rule 15 deliberately accommodates limitations. But the State's possession of the prosecution file is not notice that every act in it is challenged. Treating the whole trial as the occurrence would allow almost any new ground while the first action remains pending, making the effective limitation depend on how long that action lasts. Ordinary amendment controls against prejudice and delay do not perform the separate factual-connection work enacted in Rule 15(c)(2).

Felix's allegedly coerced interrogation and the circumstances of Williams's videotaped testimony are distinct episodes. None of the original grounds attempted the interrogation facts. The amended coercion ground therefore does not relate back. The related assistance theory must be handled under the same rule within the proceedings actually before the lower court. The separately resolved confrontation merits lie outside the grant.

**Operative application:** Timely amendments remain available under the applicable rules. Later amendments sharing the pleaded factual core may relate back even when they change the legal theory. Independent commencement, pendency or equitable grounds require their own established predicates; the remand must enforce the common-core rule and adjudicate any actually preserved independent ground on its own basis.

- Scarborough v. Principi is applied to distinguish completing an identified timely matter from adding a different factual wrong; its fee-specific prerequisites do not become habeas rules.
- Tiller v. Atlantic Coast Line Railroad Co. supports changing a legal theory while retaining the same identified factual occurrence.
- Artuz v. Bennett, Duncan v. Walker and Carey v. Saffold retain their distinct rules governing filing and qualifying state pendency.
- Rhines v. Weber retains bounded authority to preserve an existing timely mixed petition; it does not supply relation back for a new factual ground.
- Pliler v. Ford retains its specific judicial-act, causal-loss, diligence and lawful-remedy requirements.
- Pace v. DiGuglielmo and Dodd v. United States retain their separate state-filing and new-right timing holdings.

Post-limitations habeas amendments must be compared with the actual operative events pleaded or attempted on time. Genuine factual elaboration and new legal theories concerning those events may relate back; distinct episodes do not become timely merely because they contributed to the same trial or conviction. The lower court must apply that rule to the added theories within their actual procedural scope and preserve the separate confrontation disposition.

### 2005-06-23 — [Orff v. United States, No. 03-1566.](../records/orff-v-united-states_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

#### The statute permits qualifying joinder rather than this stand-alone action

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 390uu permits the United States to be added as a necessary defendant in a qualifying suit adjudicating a contracting entity's and the United States' rights under a federal reclamation contract. It does not itself authorize a stand-alone contract action against the United States and its agents; an asserted benefit under the contract cannot supply the missing procedural condition.

**Authority:** The unanimous Opinion of the Court supplies nine direct joins to the rule and disposition.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress authorized joinder as a necessary party defendant. That language describes adding the sovereign to a qualifying controversy between other parties, rather than creating general consent whenever relief is sought from it. The farmers now pursue a direct federal claim; the districts' earlier participation does not supply a continuing independent nonfederal controversy requiring federal joinder.

The farmers rely on the breadth of the statute's reference to suits adjudicating contractual rights, the second sentence's amenability to judgments and review, and the venue provision. Those words have substantial effect within the consent Congress initially grants. After qualifying joinder, the United States is subject to the specified adjudication and review. The venue sentence identifies a forum for that qualifying suit. Treating each sentence as an independent direct-action waiver would erase the opening procedural condition.

Meyer requires attention to the actual breadth of enacted consent; its broad sue-and-be-sued clause differs from this joinder provision. Blue Fox separates an asserted substantive obligation from consent to the action and requested relief. Idaho likewise distinguishes a defined adjudicatory consent from an additional permission asserted against the Government. These authorities require enforcing Congress's words rather than presuming that every waiver receives the narrowest conceivable construction.

Because the direct action falls outside the asserted waiver, intended-beneficiary entitlement cannot supply jurisdiction. The Court affirms on sovereign consent and leaves that beneficiary question without a merits determination. The sufficient direct-action ground also leaves unresolved whether every qualifying joinder must satisfy all of Rule 19(a), whether section 390uu permits money damages in a suit otherwise within its consent, and whether a noncontracting entity may invoke it in a qualifying joined controversy. Those questions are unnecessary to this threshold disposition; alternative statutory waivers lie outside the granted issue.

- FDIC v. Meyer is applied to honor the text and actual breadth of consent while keeping waiver distinct from substantive entitlement; its broader agency clause does not supply the different consent asserted here.
- Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc. is applied to require consent for the actual action and relief, independently of the alleged contractual obligation.
- United States v. Idaho is applied to distinguish a specified adjudicatory waiver from an additional asserted governmental obligation.

Section 390uu remains available for qualifying necessary-party joinder in reclamation-contract controversies. Its amenability and venue provisions operate within that consent. Farmers asserting contract benefits cannot use it alone to maintain the surviving stand-alone suit against the United States and its agents; substantive beneficiary entitlement remains distinct from the threshold waiver.

### 2005-06-27 — [Bell v. Thompson, No. 04-514.](../records/bell-v-thompson_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

#### Abuse of assumed authority to withhold the mandate

**Holding and operative rule:** Even assuming exceptional authority to withhold a mandate after completed certiorari review, a court abuses that authority when it leaves the parties without notice for months after its announced stay ends and then reopens the merits without a justification sufficient for that extraordinary course. The Sixth Circuit's combined unexplained delay, expired announced endpoints, earlier opportunities to consider the psychological material, and ensuing merits amendment require reversal here; nonissuance alone cannot justify indefinite reconsideration.

**Authority:** The Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, controls this rule, its application and the mandate.

**Controlling explanation:** Rule 41 establishes when a mandate issues and makes the mandate effective upon issuance. Carlisle v. United States requires inherent judicial authority to operate within governing procedural rules. Calderon v. Thompson protects the finality of completed habeas review, but its specific mandate-recall rule does not directly govern a mandate that never issued. The Court therefore evaluates the exercise of assumed authority at the actual procedural stage.

The Sixth Circuit expressly tied its stays to the completion of this Court's review. After that endpoint, it withheld the mandate for more than five months without a further order or notice. Tennessee proceeded on the announced endpoint while the appellate court later amended the merits disposition. Earlier rehearing papers prominently presented the Sultan material; its significance was not first raised only after the review process ended.

The omitted report and deposition support a serious mitigation argument. Their importance, and the fact that the mandate remained unissued, supply the strongest grounds for permitting correction. But they do not explain this prolonged silent departure from the announced procedure. The existence of a contested claim requiring further merits assessment differs from a sufficient justification for that exceptional appellate course. The conclusion rests on those circumstances together, rather than treating elapsed time alone as conclusive or requiring a finding that the mitigation claim lacks merit.

The general availability of exceptional post-certiorari withholding is assumed for this exercise-of-discretion ruling. The actual amendment cannot stand even on that favorable assumption.

- Carlisle v. United States is applied for rule-consistent inherent authority; its principle prevents a bare appeal to equitable power from displacing the governing procedure.
- Calderon v. Thompson is applied by analogy for finality and state reliance. Its miscarriage-of-justice requirements retain their defined application to actual recalls reopening completed habeas merits; an unissued mandate is a different procedural event.

The Court holds that the asserted exceptional authority to withhold a mandate was abused in the circumstances of this case. The prolonged withholding without notice, expired review endpoints, prior opportunities to present the evidence, limited nature of the proposed correction and the State's justified reliance together establish that abuse. Retained jurisdiction and nonissuance do not justify the course taken here. Rule 41 governs issuance, and the distinct statutory conditions of any further habeas proceeding remain applicable.

### 2005-06-27 — [McCreary County v. American Civil Liberties Union of Kentucky, No. 03-1693.](../records/mccreary-county-v-aclu-of-kentucky_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

The Court's two holdings receive six complete joins: Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

#### I. The whole official presentation supports preliminary protection

**Holding and operative rule:** A court evaluating an official religious display must examine its present content, setting and authorization together with relevant official acts showing its function. Government may explain religious history, but a cosmetic revision cannot cure its continuing adoption of religious doctrine; the counties' third arrangements, considered with their documented development and official adoption, sufficiently support the preliminary finding of prohibited sponsorship to sustain the injunction on the record reviewed below.

**Authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court controls through the six direct joins stated above.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini v. Felton makes the actual governmental act and function decisive. Purpose, effects and institutional relationships are evidence within that inquiry. The counties own and authorize these courthouse presentations. Freedom From Religion Foundation v. City of Marshfield requires examination of the public presentation that actually continues when an institution changes its formal arrangements. A genuine change must receive its effect, while a new description cannot settle what government is doing.

The first displays presented the Commandments alone. The counties then adopted religious resolutions and added selected materials containing religious references. Their third arrangements contain nine equally framed documents and a historical explanation. The earlier removal of the translation label and scriptural citation also matters, although it left the Commandments' text intact. These are substantive features to be weighed. Taken with the selective sequence and the resolutions then governing the exhibits' adoption, however, they support the preliminary assessment that the historical format continued the counties' official religious presentation.

The counties and the dissent regard the equal documents and explanatory statements as a sufficient historical transformation. That account gives too little force to the relationship between the actual explanation and the documented official acts adopting this presentation. History illuminates the current act; it does not permanently disable a county from lawful historical instruction. Neither a secular title nor sacred content alone determines the function. The resolutions' later repeal must therefore receive its real effect in the prospective proceedings described in Part II, rather than being treated as proof that the earlier preliminary assessment lacked support.

#### II. Continued relief must account for the actual governmental change

**Holding and operative rule:** When a governmental change bears materially on an enjoined display's continuing function, the district court must promptly assess that change through ordinary modification proceedings while maintaining interim protection confined to the identified presentation. On remand, the court must give the postargument repeal and any supported change in content, setting or official authorization their actual weight, modify or dissolve the injunction if a genuinely lawful historical function is established, and retain effective restraint only to the extent prohibited official adoption continues.

**Authority:** Part II of the Opinion of the Court has the same six direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Both county boards repealed their resolutions after argument. The former enactments remain evidence of the presentation reviewed below; they no longer supply operative official commands. Agostini requires the court to assess what government presently does, and Marshfield requires an effective cure to receive its actual legal effect. Repeal is therefore material to continued relief, although its existence alone does not settle the function of the entire presentation.

The injunction remains effective against the identified third arrangements while the district court promptly examines that change under the unified inquiry. The court should use the existing record wherever sufficient and consider supported changes through ordinary procedure. It must permit a genuine lawful cure and fit any continuing restraint to the governmental function actually established. Because the challenged act is an official presentation, effective protection reaches that presentation rather than merely shielding individual viewers. The court must proceed toward final adjudication; the preliminary order cannot become permanent by inertia. The post-repeal application and resulting remedy are factual and remedial work under the resolved standard.

#### Precedent treatment

- Agostini v. Felton is applied: the governmental act and its actual function govern, with purpose, effects and institutional relationships serving as evidence. Its modification of Lemon v. Kurtzman remains controlling rather than the independent-purpose formulation used below.
- Freedom From Religion Foundation, Inc. v. City of Marshfield is applied to continuing official presentation and effective correction. Its actual-function and cure principles govern; the private ownership and operational-separation remedy of that case remain tied to their own facts.
- Robinson v. City of Edmond preserves the distinction between historical explanation and governmental religious identification. Its civil-identifier holding does not make all public presentation of religious text unlawful.
- Stone v. Graham remains authoritative for its school-display setting. Its specific holding is not enlarged into a universal prohibition of historical displays containing the Commandments.
- Doe v. Porter, Cutter v. Wilkinson and Eulitt v. Maine Department of Education preserve their respective distinctions among official devotion, genuinely secular work and accommodation of private exercise; those distinctions remain part of the governing actual-function inquiry.

#### Law after decision

Official displays are governed by the present governmental act and function, established from the full record of content, setting, authorization and relevant official history. A county may correct its presentation genuinely, and a court must credit that correction while requiring effective protection against any prohibited function that continues. The challenged third arrangements support preliminary protection on the lower-review record. Their prospective treatment after repeal is governed by the same settled rule and the confined-remedy directions in Part II.

### 2005-06-27 — [Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer Studios Inc. v. Grokster, Ltd., No. 04-480.](../records/metro-goldwyn-mayer-studios-v-grokster_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

All nine Justices join the Court's inducement and procedural holdings. The separate Sony applications have only the joins stated in Opinion Topology and supply no controlling application or new minimum-use test.

#### I. Affirmative promotion creates responsibility for resulting infringement

**Holding and operative rule:** A distributor that distributes a device with the object of promoting copyright infringement, shown by clear expression or other affirmative steps directed to that object, is responsible for resulting direct infringement when the owner proves the culpable promotion and causal connection. Substantial lawful capability defeats culpability inferred from distribution alone, but does not immunize this proved inducement.

**Authority:** The unanimous Opinion of the Court directly supports this rule, its safeguards and its application to the categorical immunity asserted here.

**Controlling explanation:** Sony protects the distribution of useful technology from liability inferred merely from its capability for misuse. Kalem recognizes responsibility for purposeful promotion of infringing use. Napster likewise distinguishes protected capability from knowing material assistance to identified infringement. Together those principles permit an inducement claim based on the distributor's own affirmative conduct rather than the mere provision of a product.

Decentralized architecture may affect the ability to assist or supervise particular transfers. It does not remove the distributor's responsibility for deliberately promoting infringement resulting from distribution with that object. The record's supported promotion, assistance and business evidence must therefore receive legal effect; lawful capability alone cannot sustain the distributors' judgment.

The distributors' strongest objection is that an intent inquiry could penalize ordinary innovation because users infringe. The rule requires affirmative culpable promotion and resulting infringement under the ordinary civil burden. Ordinary distribution, neutral technical support, product updates, lawful advertising and general awareness of misuse are insufficient by themselves. Infringement volume, advertising revenue and nonfiltering may bear on purpose with other supported affirmative evidence, but none independently supplies the required conduct and connection. The rule creates no freestanding monitoring or redesign duty. Patent inducement provisions illustrate the distinction; the copyright ground rests on the recognized purposeful-promotion principle.

#### II. Earlier evidence may illuminate current distribution without enlarging judgment scope

**Holding and operative rule:** Earlier conduct may show the purpose and character of current software distribution when the record supplies an actual connection; its date alone does not exclude its evidentiary use. Review of the current-version judgment nevertheless remains distinct from adjudication of damages for excluded past versions or activities, and each party's motion and each distributor's conduct must receive separate application of the summary-judgment standard.

**Authority:** All nine Justices adopt this application and remand instruction.

**Controlling explanation:** The distributors argue that a conduct-based theory falls outside review because the lower courts confined the judgment to current software. That argument confuses the scope of liability adjudicated with the evidence relevant to its purpose. An earlier promotional act may illuminate the object of continuing distribution without making damages for that earlier act part of this judgment. The required actual connection prevents old conduct from automatically establishing current culpability or causation.

An unpublished promotional draft can evidence purpose, but its development does not prove publication or a resulting infringing transfer. Nor does the rule require a published message reaching every user: the culpable conduct may consist of distribution with the established infringing object. Resulting infringement must still be causally connected to that conduct.

The district court's direct-infringement conclusion is already established and was uncontested on appeal. The remaining inquiry concerns each defendant's secondary responsibility and its causal and remedial scope. The owners' cross-motion must be reconsidered under the correct rule, with the inferences appropriate to that motion; vacating the distributors' categorical defense neither grants the owners' motion nor requires trial of every issue. Summary judgment remains available where the actual record warrants it.

#### III. Independent secondary theories and effective relief retain their own predicates

**Holding and operative rule:** Knowing material assistance to identified infringement remains a distinct contributory theory, and vicarious liability requires both direct financial benefit and a right and practical ability to supervise. Relief must address proved infringement and culpable conduct through feasible measures that stop any established continuing inducement practice and its supported effects while preserving lawful operation; an equally effective narrower measure can satisfy that obligation.

**Authority:** The unanimous Court applies Napster's separate liability and remedial rules.

**Controlling explanation:** Napster's supervisory ground rested on actual account exclusion and index control. Those capacities cannot be inferred for these decentralized distributors from advertising receipts or theoretical power to cease distributing software. The inducement rule supplies a separate ground for responsibility, not the missing supervisory fact.

The lower court must identify the conduct and infringement attributable to each defendant before setting monetary or injunctive relief. A continuing unlawful practice requires a remedy reaching that practice and its supported effects. Defendants may establish that narrower measures fully cure the injury while preserving lawful uses. Feasibility, effective protection and lawful operation guide the remedial terms; sharing software for lawful and unlawful files alone cannot justify a blanket technology prohibition. Any actually invoked statutory service protection must be considered under its own conditions and remedial limits.

#### IV. The separate Sony application has no controlling resolution

**Existing controlling rule:** Genuine current and prospective substantial lawful capability protects against liability inferred from product distribution alone. Present infringement percentages are not the exclusive measure; Napster's independent knowing-assistance route and today's inducement rule remain available on their own facts.

**Authority and unresolved scope:** The Court unanimously preserves that capability rule. Ginsburg and Kennedy would reject the distributors' favorable Sony determination because material dispute remains; Breyer, Stevens and O'Connor would sustain that determination. Neither product-specific application has five joins, and the opposing applications cannot be combined as narrower versions of one rule. The separate distribution claim therefore remains available without a new Court resolution of this contested showing or a new minimum-use threshold.

**Record limits:** The survey classified available files rather than actual downloads. Its residual ten percent was unknown, not established lawful use; copyrighted status likewise did not alone prove unauthorized use. Identified authorized and public-domain uses and their prospective development must be distinguished from an unsupported numerical conclusion. The differing legal evaluations of those qualified facts appear in the separate writings.

- Sony Corp. of America v. Universal City Studios, Inc. retains its protection against imputing culpability from useful product distribution alone, including genuine prospective lawful capability. Affirmative inducement falls outside that protection.
- Kalem Co. v. Harper Brothers supplies the recognized purposeful-promotion principle, applied to software distribution with an infringing object and resulting infringement.
- A&M Records, Inc. v. Napster, Inc. is extended in principle from knowing material assistance to the separately supported inducement route; its particular knowledge-and-assistance, practical-supervision, statutory-protection and feasible-relief rules remain distinct.

A distributor may be held responsible for resulting infringement when affirmative conduct establishes an object of promoting that infringement and the required causal connection. Product capability and network architecture do not immunize that conduct. Ordinary distribution and neutral support remain protected against liability on those facts alone. Independent distribution, knowing-assistance and vicarious theories retain their own predicates; the disputed product-specific Sony application has no controlling resolution. Further proceedings apply the announced rule separately to the parties' motions, defendants, relevant distribution and requested relief.

### 2005-06-27 — [National Cable & Telecommunications Association v. Brand X Internet Services, No. 04-277; FCC v. Brand X Internet Services, No. 04-281.](../records/national-cable-telecommunications-association-v-brand-x-internet-services_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

The five Justices joining Parts II through IV of the Court's opinion directly support those holdings and the judgment in both dockets; eight Justices directly support Holding I. Agreement rests on the enacted definitions, the actual delegation and the force of the prior judicial construction, not on a policy preference about broadband regulation.

#### I. Chevron and Mead govern only within actual delegated authority

**Holding and operative rule:** A reviewing court gives controlling force to an agency's construction of a statute it administers only when Congress delegated the relevant interpretive choice, the agency exercised that authority through a mechanism Congress authorized to carry the force of law, and the construction stays within the statutory limits. The court decides independently whether a delegation exists and how far it extends, what the statutory limits are, whether the agency actually exercised the delegated authority, and whether procedural and reasoned-decisionmaking requirements are met. The Communications Act delegates to the FCC the administration of these service definitions, and its nationwide declaratory ruling exercises that authority; whether the classification stays within the statutory limits is decided in Holdings II and III.

**Controlling explanation:** Mead requires identification of the legal assignment and the mechanism actually exercising it. Sections 151 and 201(b), read within the Act and the implementing authority recognized in Iowa Utilities Board, entrust the Commission with administering these service definitions. The nationwide declaratory ruling exercises that authority. It differs from Mead's revisable, transaction-specific Customs letter, and formal procedure is evidence of exercise, neither necessary nor sufficient by itself.

Expertise, responsibility for a complex field or the presence of ambiguity does not alone authorize every interpretation an agency prefers. Barnhart and Yellow Transportation sustain genuine delegated choices within legal boundaries, and MCI enforces the limits of a delegated power against a reading that would remove a statutory requirement's essential operation. Chevron is retained on those terms: a court gives effect to a reasonable choice Congress actually left to the agency and enforces the line Congress drew. Mead, Barnhart and MCI remain governing law. The present classification must still satisfy the statutory limits.

#### II. An authoritative judicial construction binds within the judicial hierarchy

**Holding and operative rule:** An authoritative judicial construction of a statute's legal content binds within the judicial hierarchy that issued it until Congress changes the statute or a court with authority to do so reconsiders the construction: a court of appeals' construction binds within its circuit, and this Court's construction binds throughout the federal system. The absence of an express statement that the statute is unambiguous does not strip the construction of that authority. A binding construction of statutory meaning differs from a judicial recognition that Congress delegated a range of choice: the agency must comply with the former and may change policy within the latter. Where a court of appeals has actually settled a classification, an agency that departs from it must show that the earlier decision left an identifiable delegated choice open. The remedy for a mistaken precedent is judicial reconsideration, by the court of appeals en banc or by this Court; the agency cannot extinguish its force.

**Controlling explanation:** Chevron gives effect to Congress's allocation of genuine interpretive choice; it does not make a court's completed construction of statutory meaning provisional. The question is what the earlier court actually decided, examined by substance rather than by a search for a formula. If the decision recognized a range Congress left to the agency, the agency may choose within it. If the decision fixed the statute's legal content, the agency is bound within that hierarchy and must seek judicial reconsideration to change it. Lechmere requires a later agency approach to respect a governing judicial statutory construction, and Neal held that a later administrative measure did not displace Chapman's construction of §841. Today's rule extends that principle to agency interpretations generally, at the reach stated, while preserving actual delegated choice.

Portland construed §153(46) and held that the cable provider's transmission to its subscribers is a telecommunications service. It identified no delegated choice left open on that classification, and the Commission has not shown that one was. The Ninth Circuit therefore correctly treated Portland as binding. The question requires decision because Portland is the Ninth Circuit's ground and the petitioners expressly seek authority to displace it. This Court is not bound by Portland and reaches the same classification independently in Holding III.

The dissent contends that this rule lets the first court to construe a statute exhaust a choice Congress gave the agency. It does not. A decision that leaves a delegated range open constrains no choice within that range, and a decision that fixes statutory meaning remains subject to reconsideration by the courts Congress empowered to review it. The contrary rule would make adjudicated meaning yield to each later administrative preference and would make a circuit's law depend on whether its earlier court used a particular adjective.

#### III. The publicly offered transmission function is telecommunications service

**Holding and operative rule:** Telecommunications is transmission, between or among points the customer specifies, of information of the customer's choosing, without change in form or content; a telecommunications service is the offering of telecommunications for a fee directly to the public or to a class effectively available to the public. An information service is the offering of information capabilities via telecommunications, and its exclusion of capabilities used to manage, control or operate a telecommunications system or manage a telecommunications service applies. Bundling information capabilities with an independently offered transmission function does not extinguish the transmission's statutory character; the question is what the provider actually offers. Email, storage or similar features do not transform the transmission, and separate itemization is not required. The decisive question is whether customers are substantively offered public transmission for a fee. Cable-modem providers offer their subscribers that transmission, so the FCC's classification is set aside insofar as it excludes the publicly offered transmission function from telecommunications-service treatment.

**Controlling explanation:** Sections 153(43) and (46) focus on transmission, the customer's choice of content and destination, and an offering to the public for a fee, not on separate billing or a stand-alone sale. The subscriber pays for the ability to send and receive information of the subscriber's choosing between points the subscriber selects across the cable network, and that information arrives unchanged. The Commission's paragraphs 38–41 describe genuine retrieval, storage, email and access capabilities, and those capabilities are information services under section 153(20). They coexist with the offered transmission; they do not change the form or content of what the subscriber sends and receives. A telephone service does not escape Title II by being packaged with voicemail, and the cable transmission function does not escape it by being packaged with email. The management exclusion keeps routine network operations on the telecommunications side; it cannot turn an offered transmission service into an information service.

AT&T Corp. v. City of Portland described the offering as an information-service function provided together with a telecommunications pipeline, and the Court adopts that account. The line between an offering and an input preserves Congress's distinction: an information-service provider that merely uses telecommunications obtained from another carrier does not thereby offer telecommunications to the public. The holding classifies the transmission a facilities-based provider sells to its subscribers, not every information service that uses transmission.

The Commission's construction cannot be sustained as a choice within delegated ambiguity. The delegation recognized in Holding I does not extend to excluding from the statutory category a function the definitions cover, and complexity and the Commission's policy objectives do not enlarge the range Congress left open. Gulf Power reserved this classification; this holding resolves it. Under section 153(44), common-carrier treatment attaches only to the extent the provider furnishes telecommunications service, which here means the transmission function; the accompanying information capabilities remain information services.

#### IV. The Commission retains forbearance and implementation authority within the statutory category

**Holding and operative rule:** Classifying the transmission function as telecommunications service does not apply every Title II obligation permanently. Section 160(a) directs the Commission to forbear from applying a regulation or provision of the Act to a telecommunications carrier or service, or a class of them, in any or some geographic markets, when it determines that enforcement is not necessary to ensure just, reasonable and not unjustly or unreasonably discriminatory charges, practices, classifications or regulations, is not necessary to protect consumers, and that forbearance is consistent with the public interest, considering competitive effects under section 160(b). The Commission may forbear on those findings and must forbear when it makes them, subject to section 160(d)'s limitation for sections 251(c) and 271, itself subject to section 251(f). It may not redefine a covered service out of the statutory category to avoid those conditions.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress addressed the regulatory consequences of classification through forbearance, not through an exemption from the definitions. MCI holds that a power to modify statutory requirements does not permit removing their essential operation for a regulated class; a classification removing a covered service from Title II would accomplish without findings what section 160 permits only on them. Iowa Utilities Board preserves the Commission's national authority to implement the Act, and that authority remains available within the statutory category. Potential Title I ancillary authority does not automatically supply every common-carrier obligation for a service classified outside telecommunications service, and section 153(44)'s limit remains material.

Because the classification fails at the statutory boundary, the Court does not decide the challengers' separate objection that the Commission inadequately explained its different treatment of cable and telephone-company DSL. The Commission may address material technological and market distinctions in lawful classification, implementation and forbearance proceedings, on its own findings. The judgment orders no particular access regime, rates, net-neutrality rules or penalties. The Court resolves the challenged 2002 cable classification and the authority governing its implementation.

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc. is retained, subject to actual delegation: courts give effect to a reasonable choice Congress actually left to the agency and independently enforce statutory boundaries.
- United States v. Mead Corp. is preserved: controlling force requires actual delegation and an authorized force-of-law mechanism; this nationwide declaratory mechanism differs from the revisable Customs letters, and formality remains evidence rather than a necessary or sufficient condition.
- Barnhart v. Walton and Yellow Transportation, Inc. v. Michigan are preserved for the distinction between a permissible delegated construction and the only construction Congress permits.
- MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co. is applied: a power to modify statutory requirements does not permit the Commission to remove a covered service from the statutory category.
- FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp. is preserved: an agency cannot obtain authority by construing broad terms to reach what the connected statutory requirements do not permit.
- AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board is preserved for the Commission's national implementing authority, subject to the limits on particular rules.
- Lechmere, Inc. v. NLRB is preserved: a later agency approach must respect a governing judicial statutory construction.
- Neal v. United States is extended: its holding that a later administrative measure did not displace Chapman's construction of §841 supports the binding force of an authoritative judicial construction within the judicial hierarchy, subject to an identifiable delegated choice the construction left open. Neal's particular mixture-weight construction is unchanged.
- National Cable & Telecommunications Association v. Gulf Power Co. retains its pole-attachment holdings; its reserved Internet-service classification is resolved for the offered transmission function.
- Smiley v. Citibank preserves adequately explained interpretive change within lawful authority; the challengers' cable/DSL explanation objection is not decided.
- AT&T Corp. v. City of Portland binds within the Ninth Circuit, and its account of an information-service function provided with a telecommunications pipeline is adopted.

Controlling force for an agency's statutory construction requires an actual delegation of the relevant choice, its exercise through an authorized force-of-law mechanism and a construction within the statutory limits, each decided independently by the reviewing court. An authoritative judicial construction of statutory meaning binds within the judicial hierarchy that issued it, a court of appeals' construction within its circuit and this Court's throughout the federal system, until lawfully displaced; an agency departing from an appellate court's settled classification must show that the earlier decision left an identifiable delegated choice open, and the remedy for a mistaken precedent is judicial reconsideration. A provider offers telecommunications service when customers are substantively offered public transmission of information of their choosing between points they specify, for a fee; bundling information capabilities with that independently offered transmission does not change its statutory character, and separate itemization is not required. The FCC's classification of cable-modem service is set aside insofar as it excludes the publicly offered transmission function. The Commission keeps its implementation authority and must forbear under section 160 when it makes the required findings, but it cannot redefine a covered service out of the statutory category. No access regime, rates, net-neutrality rules or penalties are ordered.

### 2005-06-27 — [Town of Castle Rock v. Gonzales, No. 04-278.](../records/town-of-castle-rock-v-gonzales_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

#### I. DeShaney's limit is retained, and this claim rests on a state-created entitlement

**Holding and operative rule:** The Due Process Clause imposes no general constitutional duty on the State to protect individuals against private violence. That limit is retained, together with the distinct doctrines governing custody, an official's affirmative deprivation of available protection and discriminatory denial of protection. DeShaney does not decide whether state law has created an individualized entitlement to protective services; procedural due process protects such an entitlement on its own terms.

**Authority:** Part I of Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** DeShaney v. Winnebago County Department of Social Services rejects a constitutional duty to protect against private harm outside custody, and nothing here enlarges that duty. S.S. ex rel. Jervis v. McMullen's actual-custody rule, Pinder v. Johnson's distinction between omission and an official's own deprivation, the affirmative-interference rule of Saenz v. Heldenfels Brothers, and the prohibition on discriminatory denial of protection applied in Nabozny v. Podlesny and recognized in United States v. Morrison keep their separate predicates. Gonzales does not rest this claim on any of them. She alleges that Colorado law secured to her a defined protective service and that the Town, by policy, deprived her of it without due process. Doe v. District of Columbia recognized that DeShaney does not mean that no noncustodial person can possess an independently created entitlement. The question is therefore the one Board of Regents v. Roth asks of any state-created interest: whether independent law secures the benefit claimed.

#### II. A qualifying individualized protective entitlement is property, and Colorado's statute and order establish one

**Holding and operative rule:** State law creates a property interest in protective services, protected by procedural due process, when it establishes all four of these elements: (1) an identified individual or specifically defined beneficiary; (2) a sufficiently definite legal entitlement to protective services, rather than an expectation of ordinary law enforcement; (3) objective triggering conditions; and (4) material legal constraints on officials' authority to withhold the benefit once those conditions exist. The statute, any individualized order and authoritative state constructions are read together, and a protective order is not automatically sufficient. The benefit need not be transferable, monetizable or a guarantee of outcome. A general duty to investigate, discretionary promises and internal guidelines ordinarily create no such entitlement, and no entitlement guarantees prevention of every injury. Colorado's protective-order statute and Gonzales's individualized order establish each element; the entitlement's legal existence is resolved.

**Authority:** Part II of Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Board of Regents v. Roth protects a legitimate claim of entitlement that independent law secures, not a unilateral expectation. Sandin v. Conner, applied this Term in Wilkinson v. Austin, asks whether substantive law meaningfully restricts officials' authority rather than whether its words are mandatory; that sufficient, nonexclusive principle informs state-created interests generally, although its prison application does not transfer. An identified beneficiary and objective triggers distinguish a secured individual benefit from the public's general interest in enforcement. Definiteness distinguishes a defined protective service from ordinary policing or a general duty to investigate. Material constraints on withholding supply the substantive limit Sandin requires.

Colorado's scheme meets each element. The order names the protected person. The statute requires every reasonable means to enforce the order, and it requires arrest, or a warrant when arrest is impractical, when officers have information amounting to probable cause both that the restrained person violated or attempted to violate the order and that the person was properly served or had actual notice of its existence and substance. Those predicates are objective, and the commands constrain refusal once they exist. The protected object is the protective service itself: enforcement of the order on the beneficiary's behalf. That benefit is distinct from the procedures used to decide whether it is owed, so the claim does not seek process for its own sake. Doe v. District of Columbia's investigative-procedure holding concerned steps preceding any commanded protection, and its reservation of independently created entitlements describes this scheme.

The order's parenting permissions and reasonable-notice condition define when a violation occurs; they do not dissolve the entitlement. The reverse-side notice does not itself bind police; the statute supplies the obligation. Gonzaga University v. Doe concerns statutory rights enforceable under §1983, and that inquiry remains distinct from constitutional property. No dispositive unresolved Colorado construction requires certification.

#### III. Enforcement discretion is preserved, and a required response is not a guaranteed outcome

**Holding and operative rule:** The entitlement leaves officers' enforcement judgment intact. Under Colorado's statute officers still determine, using reasonable means, whether a violation or attempted violation occurred, whether probable cause exists as to the violation and as to service or actual notice, and whether immediate arrest is impractical amid competing emergencies, in which case they must seek a warrant. They lack only unrestricted discretion to disregard the obligation once its conditions exist. The beneficiary is entitled to the required response, not to a particular tactic, a successful arrest or the prevention of harm.

**Authority:** Part III of Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Discretion over means differs from discretion to refuse a duty. Colorado's reasonable-means command preserves practical judgment about how and when to act, and the warrant alternative accommodates circumstances that make arrest impractical. The probable-cause predicates require officers to assess the information presented, including the order's parenting permissions and notice condition, before the duty attaches. Recognizing the entitlement therefore does not make officers insurers, convert every enforcement choice into a constitutional question or allow a beneficiary to demand prosecution of every offense. A general tradition of police discretion cannot nullify a statute that removes the choice to provide no reasonable enforcement at all once its conditions are met. Where no comparable entitlement exists, ordinary enforcement discretion is untouched. Whether the predicates existed on the night in question is a matter of proof under this rule, not a reason to deny the entitlement.

#### IV. Mathews governs the process due, without a mandatory list or emergency hearing

**Holding and operative rule:** The process due for this entitlement is determined under Mathews v. Eldridge by weighing the beneficiary's interest, the risk of erroneous deprivation and the value of additional safeguards, and the governmental interest, including the burdens of emergency policing. Adequate process may include an accessible means of requesting enforcement, prompt consideration, supervisory review, communication of a refusal and timely reconsideration, but none of these is constitutionally required in every case, and no formal predeprivation hearing is required in an emergency. Parratt v. Taylor and Hudson v. Palmer continue to govern random, unauthorized failures for which predeprivation process is impracticable and adequate postdeprivation remedies exist. An alleged established municipal policy of refusing meaningful consideration of qualifying requests states a procedural due-process claim.

**Authority:** Part IV of Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Wilkinson v. Austin approved designed safeguards only as they function meaningfully, and it declined to require procedures whose added value did not justify their cost. Mathews calls for the same practical inquiry here. Protective enforcement is time-sensitive, and a hearing that delayed action would defeat the interest it protects.
