The safeguards with real value let a beneficiary present the order and the information bearing on its conditions, receive a genuine assessment and learn of a refusal in time to seek reconsideration. Zinermon v. Burch distinguishes an established procedure or foreseeable policy from an unauthorized departure: when the deprivation results from the municipality's own policy, predeprivation safeguards are feasible and a postdeprivation remedy does not satisfy due process. Connecticut Department of Public Safety v. Doe requires that additional process concern facts material under the governing rule; the statutory conditions are such facts. Repeated contacts with officers neither establish nor disprove meaningful consideration under a pleaded policy that prevents it. The district court determines what the Town's procedures provided and what additional safeguards were practicable.

#### V. Monell, causation and Carey govern municipal liability and damages

**Holding and operative rule:** The Town is liable only if its own policy or custom caused an actual deprivation of the entitlement without the process due; respondeat superior does not apply. Causation of the children's deaths is not presumed from the existence of the duty or from a procedural violation. Under Carey v. Piphus, a proved denial of process supports nominal damages, and compensatory damages require proof of actual injury caused by that denial.

**Authority:** Part V of Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Monell v. Department of Social Services requires the municipality's own policy or custom to be the moving force behind the constitutional deprivation, and Saenz v. Heldenfels Brothers kept that attribution requirement separate from individual defenses. Gonzales alleges a policy tolerating nonenforcement; that allegation is assumed at the dismissal stage and must be proved. Carey separates the constitutional violation from compensable injury. A denial of process is actionable for nominal damages without proof of other harm, but compensation requires proof that the injury resulted from the denial of process rather than from a deprivation that would have occurred with adequate process. The deaths cannot automatically measure damages merely because an enforcement duty existed, and an unsuccessful protective effort does not by itself establish either the violation or the injury.

- DeShaney v. Winnebago County Department of Social Services is preserved: there is no general constitutional duty to protect against private violence, and its rule does not address a state-created entitlement.
- Pinder v. Johnson and Saenz v. Heldenfels Brothers are preserved: their affirmative-deprivation rules keep their own predicates, and Saenz's separation of municipal attribution from individual defenses is applied.
- Doe v. District of Columbia is preserved: its holding that mandatory investigative procedures alone create no substantive entitlement is unchanged, and its reservation of independently created entitlements is developed by the qualifying-entitlement rule.
- Sandin v. Conner is preserved and applied for its sufficient, nonexclusive substantive-constraint principle, without transplanting its prison application; Wilkinson v. Austin is applied for safeguards that function meaningfully.
- Board of Regents v. Roth, Mathews v. Eldridge and Monell v. Department of Social Services are applied.
- Parratt v. Taylor and Hudson v. Palmer are preserved for random, unauthorized deprivations; Zinermon v. Burch is applied to the pleaded established policy, and Carey v. Piphus to damages.
- Connecticut Department of Public Safety v. Doe retains its requirement that additional process concern facts material under the governing substantive law.
- Gonzaga University v. Doe is distinguished: statutory rights enforceable under §1983 and constitutional property remain distinct inquiries.

A qualifying individualized protective entitlement is property protected by procedural due process when state law identifies the beneficiary, defines a sufficiently definite entitlement to protective services, sets objective triggers and materially constrains officials' authority to withhold the service once triggered; Colorado's protective-order statute and an individualized order establish such an entitlement. DeShaney's limit, the custody, affirmative-deprivation and discriminatory-denial doctrines, and Doe's investigative-procedure holding remain. Officers retain discretion over reasonable means and over the probable-cause and practicability judgments the statute commits to them, but not discretion to disregard the obligation; the entitlement is to the required response, not a guaranteed outcome. Mathews determines the process due without a mandatory list of safeguards or a formal emergency hearing, and Parratt and Hudson govern random, unauthorized failures. Municipal liability requires the municipality's own policy or custom causing the procedural deprivation, and Carey separates the violation from compensable injury.

### 2005-06-27 — [Van Orden v. Perry, No. 03-1500.](../records/van-orden-v-perry_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

The Court's two holdings receive five complete joins: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Ginsburg.

#### I. The State's presentation adopts the religious commands

**Holding and operative rule:** Government may preserve and explain religious history, but may not present theological commands as its own authoritative civic message. Texas's complete presentation of this monument, including its attribution, surroundings and visitor materials, continues that prohibited governmental adoption and violates the Establishment Clause.

**Authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court controls the actual-function rule and its application through the five direct joins stated above.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini v. Felton requires examination of the governmental act and the function it performs. A secular civic objective cannot justify using public authority to adopt religious doctrine. Robinson v. City of Edmond distinguishes actual historical exposition from making religious content an authoritative civil identifier. Freedom From Religion Foundation v. City of Marshfield requires attention to the continuing public presentation rather than nominal descriptions of ownership or purpose.

Texas selected and maintains this permanent monument on the Capitol grounds. The gift attribution, other civic memorials, visitor brochure, signs and trial-video account are genuine evidence supporting the State's description of an integrated historical presentation. The Court gives that evidence its stated force. Yet general commemoration of civic ideals and identification of a donated artifact do not sufficiently change the present function of these prominently displayed theological commands. In this setting, the State retains them as authoritative civic instruction rather than adequately making their historical place the object of explanation.

The dissent weighs the same complete context differently, treating the donated monument as a preserved source within a civic collection. That is the central disagreement. The surrounding historical apparatus supplies context, but does not resolve how this particular religious text functions in the State's continuing presentation. Neither the age of the monument nor absence of compelled participation removes the independent prohibition on governmental adoption. Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette protects genuinely private expression; the State's permanent selected display has a different source and function.

#### II. The remedy must end the prohibited governmental presentation

**Holding and operative rule:** The district court must provide prospective relief that effectively ends the unconstitutional governmental function. It may select an effective lawful change in presentation rather than require removal if that change fully supplies the relief to which Van Orden is entitled; a nominal adjustment leaving the prohibited function intact is insufficient.

**Authority:** Part II has the same five direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Marshfield makes the actual resulting presentation decisive when a government proposes a cure. Removing the monument from the official presentation or incorporating it into a genuinely explanatory historical arrangement can therefore be considered on the remedial record. The judicial task is to test the arrangement's actual function and ensure that the violation ends. Religious content alone does not defeat a genuine historical exhibit, and preserving an object does not establish that the State has ceased adopting its commands. The district court must choose and enforce an effective lawful measure in light of the actual proposal.

- Agostini v. Felton is applied as the unified governmental-act-and-function inquiry. Its modification of Lemon remains controlling; civic purpose cannot excuse prohibited governmental adoption.
- Robinson v. City of Edmond is applied for the distinction between historical explanation and official religious identification; its civil-badge application is not converted into a categorical monument rule.
- Freedom From Religion Foundation v. City of Marshfield is applied to the actual continuing presentation and effectiveness of a proposed cure; its private-ownership facts do not decide this State-owned display.
- Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette is distinguished because protection for genuine private expression does not determine the constitutional character of the State's own selected presentation.

Official religious displays remain governed by the actual governmental function. Courts must assess the complete presentation, including real explanatory materials, and distinguish preservation of religious history from adoption of religious propositions through public authority. Texas's present arrangement crosses that boundary. Prospective relief must change that function effectively while permitting a genuinely lawful historical presentation.

## Court and standing procedure

### Court and seniority

All nine seats are occupied at the opening of OT2004. The Supreme Court Composition register controls the roster, service dates and seniority. All nine Justices are seated before OT2004 opens, subject to established case-specific nonparticipation; no vacancy or new oath-date participation condition applies.

| Seniority | Justice | Office | Seat | Supreme Court service began | Current office began | Source and effective date |
|---:|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela | Chief Justice | Chief Justice line | October 7, 1991 | October 7, 1991 | Composition register; opening substitution effective October 7, 1991. |
| 2 | John Paul Stevens | Senior Associate Justice | Stevens line | December 19, 1975 | December 19, 1975 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 3 | Sandra Day O'Connor | Associate Justice | O'Connor line | September 25, 1981 | September 25, 1981 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 4 | Antonin Scalia | Associate Justice | Scalia line | September 26, 1986 | September 26, 1986 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 5 | Anthony M. Kennedy | Associate Justice | Kennedy line | February 18, 1988 | February 18, 1988 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 6 | David H. Souter | Associate Justice | Souter line | October 9, 1990 | October 9, 1990 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 7 | Clarence Thomas | Associate Justice | Marshall line | October 23, 1991 | October 23, 1991 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 8 | Ruth Bader Ginsburg | Associate Justice | White line | August 10, 1993 | August 10, 1993 | Composition register; seated at the OT2004 opening. |
| 9 | Stephen G. Breyer | Most-junior Associate Justice | Blackmun line | August 3, 1994 | August 3, 1994 | Composition register; took seat August 3, 1994. |

**Opinion-assignment seniority:** For each judgment component, the Chief Justice assigns when he is in the judgment majority. Otherwise the most senior participating Associate Justice in that majority assigns, in this order: Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

### Circuit allotments

The order effective August 3, 1994 governs at the OT2004 opening.

| Circuit | Allotted Justice | Effective date | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| District of Columbia Circuit | Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela, Chief Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| First Circuit | Stephen G. Breyer, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Second Circuit | Ruth Bader Ginsburg, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Third Circuit | David H. Souter, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |

| Eighth Circuit | Sandra Day O'Connor, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Ninth Circuit | Anthony M. Kennedy, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Tenth Circuit | Sandra Day O'Connor, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Eleventh Circuit | Clarence Thomas, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Federal Circuit | Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela, Chief Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |

**Allotment rule:** A membership change triggers a complete reallotment. No Justice inherits a predecessor's circuits. Under the Composition register, Stone-Zsela reassesses the entire map for institutional effectiveness; an express administrative order may also change an allotment.

### Standing practice

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991; signed explanations and certification, effective October 2, 1995.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). From October 2, 1995, referral does not prevent the Circuit Justice, or the substitute the term-effective Rules designate, from taking a necessary temporary action in his or her own name while the Court considers the application. Each action is explained in short signed writings, including the Circuit Justice's own explanation at final disposition, whether prevailing or dissenting, and a statement by each participating Justice in each Court action deciding relief, and is certified for issuance by the Chief Justice or the Chief's substitute, as Engine §10 provides. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice. Source: the OT1991 opening practice carried in the opening Standing State and the OT1993 Term Continuity Note. Source of the October 2, 1995 revision: user instruction.

All 105 neutral Participation Matter fields identify no case-specific nonparticipation. The opening roster therefore carries no established case-specific absence. Recheck participation at the relevant milestones during Run. A later-reported historical absence remains a verification flag until established for this Court; it does not create an opening conflict.

The [manifest](manifest.md) preserves all 105 scheduled events. Its 27 granted matters match Standing State once each. Kansas v. Colorado, No. 105, Original, completed the scheduled December 7 exceptions event and remains an open original action. Compensation and decree preparation are recommitted under the binding 1969 loss-cohort method; the six resolved exceptions, retained federal review and concrete return route govern further work. No final dollar total or terminal decree has been entered.

The three other carried originals have no scheduled OT2004 event:

| Case or matter | Stage at opening | OT2004 scheduling | Status | Source in Standing State |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Arizona v. California*, No. 8, Original | Supplemental decree in force; original jurisdiction retained; unresolved Fort Yuma title, cession, boundary, irrigable-acreage and water-quantity issues before the Special Master. | No OT2004 event date is scheduled. | Open carryover | Supplemental-decree Decision Record, October 10, 2000. |
| *Nebraska v. Wyoming*, No. 108, Original | Further litigation-cost allocation pending; settled merits claims dismissed with prejudice. | No OT2004 event date is scheduled. | Open carryover | Order and modified-decree Decision Record, November 13, 2001. |
| *Kansas v. Nebraska*, No. 126, Original, with Colorado | Limited model-completion proceedings; covered pre-December 15, 2002 claims, counterclaims and cross-claims await dismissal with prejudice upon the Master's final report certifying the States' adoption of the RRCA Groundwater Model. | No OT2004 event date is scheduled. | Open carryover | Settlement-approval and conditional-decree Decision Record, May 19, 2003. |

The manifest carries the conditional same-term checks from neutral intake. They require the actual scope of any intervening effective ruling to be checked at Run, assume no result, and establish no coordinated same-day sequence. The unscheduled originals retain their recorded conditions; no report, filing, new deadline or terminal event is inferred.

Kansas v. Colorado remains open under the December 7 calculation, review and return arrangements. The completed events change no roster, allotment or standing practice.


**Completed March 7, March 22 and March 29 implementation.** The following are lower-court or administrative implementation of completed Court events; they do not create a new pending Supreme Court event without a supported return.

- **[Back v. Hastings on Hudson Union Free School District; judgment below, 365 F.3d 107.](../records/back-v-hastings-on-hudson_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection), 2005-03-07.** The case returns through the Second Circuit to the district court for trial of Back's claims against Brennan and Wishnie. The factfinder will resolve the disputed statements, performance explanations, intent and causation; any established liability will receive the damages and employment-relief analysis the proved injury supports.

- **[Ballard v. Commissioner; No. 03-184; consolidated with Estate of Kanter v. Commissioner, No. 03-1034.](../records/ballard-v-commissioner_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection), 2005-03-07.** Both cases return through their respective Courts of Appeals to the Tax Court for the disclosure, response and review process specified in Judgment & Remedy. Any ensuing appellate review must proceed on a record that permits enforcement of Rule 183's prescribed treatment of the submitted findings.

- **[Shepard v. United States; No. 03-9168.](../records/shepard-v-united-states_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection), 2005-03-07.** The case returns through the First Circuit for sentencing under Parts I and II. The district court must make the predicate-by-predicate documentary, charging, admission, preservation and sentencing determinations described above and enter a sentence within the resulting lawful authority.

- **[Wilkinson v. Dotson; No. 03-287; claims of William Dwight Dotson and William Johnson.](../records/wilkinson-v-dotson_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection), 2005-03-07.** The district court must resolve the restored claims, applicable defenses and any supported prospective remedy on the developed record. If a violation warrants a new proceeding, the relief must preserve the lawful parole decision entrusted to the responsible officials and remain enforceable through ordinary injunction procedures.

- **[Black Political Task Force v. Galvin; lower-court No. 02-11190; 300 F. Supp. 2d 291.](../records/black-political-task-force-v-galvin_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection), 2005-03-22.** The affirmed Count I declaration and permanent injunction remain effective. Election officials continue under enacted Plan C, subject to ordinary implementation and lawful modification of the existing judgment. The statutory fee proceeding determines reasonable fees and costs. Counts II and III remain dismissed, and the conceded mootness of the remaining 2004 candidate-remedy issues stands.

- **[Brown v. Payton; No. 03-1039; 544 U.S. 133.](../records/brown-v-payton_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection), 2005-03-22.** The case returns for implementation of the reversal of penalty habeas relief on this claim. The convictions remain intact, and unrelated habeas claims proceed according to their own lawful posture.

- **[Muehler v. Mena; No. 03-1423.](../records/muehler-v-mena_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection), 2005-03-22.** The case returns to the Ninth Circuit to perform the force, duration and verdict review stated in the holdings and to order the further district-court proceedings that review requires. The mandate permits a limited new trial only where a material issue cannot be settled through the existing lawful verdict and appropriate appellate review.

- **[Rancho Palos Verdes v. Abrams; No. 03-1601.](../records/rancho-palos-verdes-v-abrams_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection), 2005-03-22.** On remand, the court must deny the requests depending exclusively on the foreclosed section 1983 claim, preserve the direct permit judgment and adjudicate any remaining properly preserved relief under the law that supplies it.

- **[City of Sherrill v. Oneida Indian Nation of New York; No. 03-855; the Lead, Eviction and Members cases at their reviewed scope.](../records/city-of-sherrill-v-oneida-indian-nation_merits_2005-03-29.md#public-projection), 2005-03-29.** The case returns for a judgment implementing rejection of the Lead tax-immunity claim and conforming treatment of the nonrevival ground. The independent Lead counterclaim, Eviction and Members immunity judgments remain operative, including the separate sales-tax damages dismissal. The other five amendment denials, discovery denial and preliminary-injunction denial remain effective. The separate Madison County proceedings continue under their existing remand.

- **[Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education; No. 02-1672; 544 U.S. 167.](../records/jackson-v-birmingham-board-of-education_merits_2005-03-29.md#public-projection), 2005-03-29.** The case returns through the Eleventh Circuit for ordinary adjudication of Jackson's individual retaliation claim. The District Court must permit development of the decisionmaking and causation record, assess the Board's supported lawful explanation and other defenses, and identify relief repairing any proved injury. A proved violation permits appropriate compensation for causal pay loss and other legally recoverable injury, together with justified prospective employment relief addressing continuing consequences.

- **[Sabree ex rel. Sabree v. Richman; review of 367 F.3d 180 (3d Cir. 2004).](../records/sabree-ex-rel-sabree-v-richman_merits_2005-03-29.md#public-projection), 2005-03-29.** The action returns for adjudication of the named beneficiaries' claims. The District Court must identify the operative benefits and waiting-list decisions; develop the timing, causes of delay, official responsibility and lawful compliance options; and decide the request for prospective relief. It must resolve the reserved financing-versus-furnishing issue under the dated payment definition and the developed record. Any representative relief requires the Rule 23 determinations and proof of a common unlawful practice. An injunction following proof must set measurable lawful assistance obligations, reasonable deadlines and adequate reporting, and must be enforced if the Secretary fails to perform. Its reach must cure the established violation, including a proved common practice for those governed by it unless a narrower cure is demonstrated.


**Completed chunk 6 implementation.** These are the mandates and further proceedings stated in the completed Court events. Lower-court or administrative implementation does not itself create another pending Supreme Court event.

### 2005-03-30 — [California Public Employees’ Retirement System v. WorldCom, Inc.](../records/california-public-employees-retirement-system-v-worldcom-inc_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall identify the covered individual Securities Act actions participating in the reviewed forum judgment and enter orders returning them to their originating state courts.

**Procedure After Action.**

The mandate returns the matter for entry and implementation of the corresponding state-court remand orders. The district court shall transmit the covered actions to their originating courts and cease merits adjudication of those actions after remand. Any independently applicable bankruptcy stay continues according to its own terms.


### 2005-03-30 — [Exxon Mobil Corp. v. Saudi Basic Industries Corp.](../records/exxon-mobil-corp-v-saudi-basic-industries-corp_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The federal proceeding is restored for adjudication of the remaining issues under their governing jurisdictional, preclusion and procedural rules.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for the remaining immunity appeal and consistent further proceedings. The lower courts must identify each live claim and requested relief, apply the Delaware judgment’s preclusive effect under §1738 and address any genuine jurisdictional or mootness defense. The Delaware judgment remains effective, and any remaining recovery must respect its binding effect and the prohibition on duplicate recovery.


### 2005-03-30 — [Rhines v. Weber](../records/rhines-v-weber_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Vacated and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The pending federal proceeding shall be retained during prompt consideration of whether the actual stay meets the Court’s standard.

**Procedure After Action.**

On remand, the lower courts shall assess Rhines’s actual reason for nonexhaustion, the potential merit of the eight claims, any abusive or intentional delay and his state-court progress. They shall evaluate the existing sixty-day conditions and impose reasonable exhaustion-and-return deadlines if a stay remains warranted. If a stay is refused, Rhines must receive a meaningful opportunity to delete the unexhausted claims where wholesale dismissal would unreasonably impair federal relief. Any dismissal must identify its actual effect on timely federal review.


### 2005-03-30 — [Smith v. City of Jackson](../records/smith-v-city-of-jackson_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Affirmed as to the disparate-impact claim, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Justices differ on whether the statute permits that cause of action, as stated in the opinions.

**Procedure After Action.**

The mandate sustains summary judgment on the disparate-impact branch. The separate intentional-treatment claim continues on its existing discovery remand under its own governing law.


### 2005-04-04 — [Consumers Energy Co. v. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission](../records/consumers-energy-v-ferc_merits_2005-04-04.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Affirmed, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

Consumers' request to set aside the authorization is denied. The challenged United States sales authorization remains effective.

**Procedure After Action.**

The denial of review stands, and FERC continues administering the authorization under its statutory oversight duties. A later claim of actual discriminatory operation must proceed through the appropriate agency channel on its own record. Under section 825l(c), rehearing or judicial review does not itself stay an order absent a specific agency or court order.


### 2005-04-04 — [Johnson v. United States](../records/johnson-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-04.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, and Ginsburg.
**Opposing:** Justices O’Connor, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer.

The lower court must apply the date on which due diligence would have disclosed the order and determine the vacatur’s material effect on the enhanced sentence under the governing requirements.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for application of the date on which due diligence would have disclosed the order and a reasoned determination of the vacatur’s scope, its connection to the enhancement and the lawful sentencing consequence under §2255. A timing dismissal may not rest solely on failure to obtain the state order earlier. If the authorized claim establishes that the enhancement lacks a required predicate, the sentence must be vacated to that extent and resentencing must proceed under the applicable sentencing law.


### 2005-04-04 — [Rousey v. Jacoway](../records/rousey-v-jacoway_merits_2005-04-04.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The accounts satisfy the similar-plan and age-connection requirements. The lower courts must determine the amount reasonably necessary for the debtors' and their dependents' support and address any remaining properly preserved account-specific objection. Turnover may reach only amounts lawfully found nonexempt.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for revision of the exemption and turnover rulings under these holdings. Before distribution to creditors, the bankruptcy court must identify any nonexempt amount and its legal basis after applying the support limit and resolving remaining properly preserved objections under Rule 4003(c).


### 2005-04-19 — [Dura Pharmaceuticals, Inc. v. Broudo](../records/dura-pharmaceuticals-v-broudo_merits_2005-04-19.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The existing inflation-only device theory is legally insufficient. Further proceedings, including consideration of any timely proposed amendment, must follow the applicable procedural rules. The separate profitability and scienter branch is outside this review.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for proceedings consistent with the causal-loss rule and the identified pleading defect. The district court must address any timely amendment under the applicable rules and either dismiss a remaining legally insufficient device claim or proceed on a sufficient claim to the proof the statute requires. The separate profitability and scienter branch retains its own procedural scope.


### 2005-04-19 — [Monsanto Co. v. McFarling](../records/monsanto-co-v-mcfarling_merits_2005-04-19.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Contract breach and categorical saved-seed/patent-scope misuse defenses | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Confined breach and defense rulings stand, subject to any legally established consequence of a separately preserved enforcement defense |
| Independent tying counterclaim | Vacated and remanded, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Apply the independent tying elements to the preserved record |
| Vacatur of the $780,000 liquidated sum and actual-damages remand | Preserved, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Actual damages proceed under the surviving lawful basis |

**Procedure After Action.**

The lower courts shall determine whether the preserved tying record independently warrants summary judgment under the stated elements and address any legally established enforcement consequence. Actual contract damages shall be assessed on the surviving lawful basis without reinstating the invalid multiplier. The unfinished patent causes, their damages and the request for injunctive relief remain in their respective proceedings under their own requirements; this reviewed partial contract judgment does not make those claims final.


### 2005-04-26 — [Doe v. Porter; Sixth Circuit Nos. 02-5316 and 02-5823, 370 F.3d 558.](../records/doe-v-porter_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Affirmed, 9–0.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The injunction against the devotional program as operated remains effective throughout the affected school system. The family-standing determination, public-pseudonymity order and challenged fee award are sustained. The Board must cease scheduling or authorizing the devotional classes as school instruction and inform the responsible principals. The district court retains authority to enforce the decree and require appropriate compliance reporting.

**Procedure After Action.**

The mandate leaves the program injunction, protective order and fee award in force. The district court supervises actual compliance and may address a concrete proposed change through ordinary proceedings. The Board must end the adjudicated devotional program while any such change is considered.

### 2005-04-26 — [Pasquantino v. United States, No. 03-725.](../records/pasquantino-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Affirmed, 5–4.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas. Opposing: Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The judgment sustains the Pasquantino brothers' six wire-fraud convictions and Hilts's Count I conviction against the statutory and revenue-rule challenge. The constitutional sentencing request first made in merits briefing is outside this review. Any independently available sentencing or restitution issue must proceed under its own authority and presentation requirements.

**Procedure After Action.**

The lower courts implement the affirmance of the convictions on the statutory issue. Independently proper sentencing or restitution matters retain their own governing law, procedural prerequisites and evidentiary burdens.

### 2005-04-26 — [Small v. United States, No. 03-750.](../records/small-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Reversed and remanded, 6–3.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.

The Japanese conviction cannot serve as this prosecution's §922(g)(1) predicate. The district court must examine the actual judgment and conditional plea, vacate the count and dependent punishment resting solely on the Japanese predicate, and conduct any remaining proceedings on their independent lawful bases.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for implementation of the reversal, removal of the conviction and sentence dependent on the Japanese predicate, and any further proceedings consistent with the domestic-category holding.

### 2005-04-27 — [Bates v. Dow Agrosciences LLC, No. 03-388; 544 U.S. 431.](../records/bates-v-dow-agrosciences-llc_merits_2005-04-27.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Vacated and remanded, 9–0.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The Court removes the inducement-based preemption barrier to genuine nonlabel duties and remands labeling-related fraud and nonwarranty DTPA theories for actual comparison with federal obligations. The instruction protecting enforceable voluntary express warranties, including promises appearing on a label, and DTPA theories insofar as they remedy those warranties is adopted 7–2. Supporting that instruction: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Opposing that instruction: Scalia and Thomas, who would require comparison of label-based warranty duties as well.

The abandoned appellate challenge concerning breach of contract and the unappealed limitation-of-remedies ruling on the materially different presale representation remain effective.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns to the Fifth Circuit for further proceedings consistent with the opinion. The lower courts must classify the actual duties, apply the surviving-warranty rule, compare the remaining labeling duties with the federal obligations applicable to the alleged conduct, and decide any preserved independently supported conflict. They must give the Appendix 192 disclaimer its ordinary procedural effect and apply the state causes' elements. Any jury instructions must confine recovery on the remaining labeling theories subject to federal comparison to substantively equivalent duties. Part III governs the surviving voluntary-warranty and warranty-derived DTPA theories. The abandoned breach-of-contract challenge and the unappealed limitation-of-remedies ruling on the materially different presale representation remain effective.

### 2005-04-27 — [Gates v. Cook; Fifth Circuit No. 03-60529, 376 F.3d 323.](../records/gates-v-cook_merits_2005-04-27.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Affirmed in part, vacated in part and remanded, 9–0.** Supporting every component: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

| Component | Disposition |
|---|---|
| Actual administrative completion and certified common prospective claim | Exhaustion defense rejected; affirmed |
| Proved conditions and supported specific protections | Affirmed at the geographic and substantive scope below |
| Paragraph 9's unrestricted incorporation of outside professional standards | Vacated; vendor duty immediately confined to necessary correction of the adjudicated mental-health and related medical violations; remanded for prompt conforming language |

The specific protections remain enforceable during remand:

| Decree paragraph | Operative treatment |
|---|---|
| 1–2 | Provide a clean receiving cell upon transfer within Unit 32-C and adequate supplies and equipment for cleaning at least weekly. The prisoner's ordinary cell-cleaning responsibility remains. |
| 4 | In Unit 32-C, measure heat index at 10 a.m., 1 p.m., 4 p.m. and 7 p.m. May–September, and at 1 p.m. otherwise. At 90 or above, provide a fan in each cell, ice water for each prisoner and one shower opportunity that day. Officials may instead provide fans, ice water and daily showers throughout May–September. |
| 5 | Continue Unit 32-C pest control and repair windows with screens of 18 gauge or better. |
| 6 | Correct Unit 32-C sewage backflow and furnish the ordered eradication plan. The existing record does not establish recalibration alone as an adequate cure. |
| 7 | Provide twenty foot-candles of lighting per Unit 32-C cell as the supported correction for the sanitation and mental-health harms. |
| 9 | Preserve private comprehensive annual mental-health examinations, separate housing for prisoners diagnosed with psychosis and severe mental illness, appropriate psychotropic-medication assessment and monitoring, and private counseling and evaluation. |
| 3, 8 and 10 | The prior vacatur of the written maintenance-program, laundry, and exercise-area sneaker, shade and water provisions remains effective. |

The district court must promptly set a schedule for the confined paragraph 9 clarification and support the resulting command under §3626. The judgment's operative limit applies immediately: professional standards may inform adequate performance of necessary care, but unrelated or later-added standards do not become enforceable merely by incorporation. The specific independent care duties continue.

**Procedure After Action.**

The district court retains reporting and enforcement authority, sets a prompt schedule for the confined paragraph 9 language, and preserves every independent protection during that work. Officials must demonstrate functioning facilities and care rather than promises to change vendors or efforts leaving the known danger unabated. Existing plan obligations continue on their existing footing. A current evidentiary record may support a properly tailored modification, including an equally effective security-sensitive means, under the governing statutory requirements.

### 2005-04-27 — [Pace v. DiGuglielmo; No. 03-9627; 544 U.S. 408.](../records/pace-v-diguglielmo_merits_2005-04-27.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Affirmed, 5–4.** Supporting: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The state application supplies no statutory exclusion, and Pace fails to establish the reasonable diligence required for his asserted equitable protection. The district court must implement dismissal of this federal application as untimely.

**Procedure After Action.**

The mandate sustains the federal limitations disposition. The district court implements dismissal of the untimely application.

### 2005-05-16 — [American Civil Liberties Union of Nevada v. Heller; Ninth Circuit No. 01-15462, 378 F.3d 979.](../records/american-civil-liberties-union-of-nevada-v-heller_merits_2005-05-16.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Judgment: Vacated and remanded, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Stevens, Thomas and Ginsburg.

The lower court must promptly apply the governing sponsor-identification rule to the named challengers' prospective claim and the presented facial challenge. It must identify the actual publication classes and financers, assess the State's justification for the required name and address exposure, and determine whether established invalid applications warrant protective, severable or facial relief. Facial sufficiency remains open under that inquiry. Vacatur of the appellate judgment does not reinstate the district court's blanket validation.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for prompt adjudication of the facial claim and the named challengers' supported prospective protection under Parts II and III. The lower court must determine fit, substantiality, lawful severability and effective relief on the existing record, supplemented only when genuinely necessary, and administer any interim protection under ordinary equitable standards.

### 2005-05-16 — [Granholm v. Heald and consolidated cases; Nos. 03-1116, 03-1120 and 03-1274; 544 U.S. 460.](../records/granholm-v-heald_merits_2005-05-16.md#public-projection)

**Judgment & Remedy.**

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No. 03-1116, Michigan governmental petition | Sixth Circuit affirmed, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Thomas | Implement judgment for the challengers against discriminatory direct-shipping access under Michigan's lawful remedial and severability rules |
| No. 03-1120, Michigan wholesaler petition | Sixth Circuit affirmed, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Thomas | Same Michigan equality remedy |
| No. 03-1274, New York | Commerce Clause disposition reversed and remanded, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, O'Connor, Thomas | Enter effective nondiscriminatory relief under New York's lawful remedial and severability rules |

Enforcement of the geographic preferences must be enjoined for the producers subject to the challenged policies. A State seeking a narrower remedy must establish that it fully eliminates the inequality. Each State may choose a lawful equal distribution arrangement, including lawful extension or withdrawal of the local direct-sale privilege, but may not continue unequal access through indefinite policy reconsideration.

The Second Circuit's independent advertising ruling and its rejection of the separate Privileges and Immunities claim retain their existing scope. Those rulings fall outside the common question presented here.

**Procedure After Action.**

The Michigan judgments proceed to implementation in favor of the challengers. The New York case returns for effective nondiscriminatory relief. The lower courts must promptly settle lawful state-specific severability and remedy, enjoin the geographic preferences, and specify the permissions and prohibitions administered by the responsible licensing agencies during implementation. Lawful equal extension or withdrawal remains subject to that state-law determination; neutral regulatory obligations continue under their actual terms. New York's independent advertising protection and Privileges and Immunities disposition retain their existing scope.

### 2005-05-23 — [Clingman v. Beaver; No. 04-37.](../records/clingman-v-beaver_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The Court affirms the judgment against the challenged exclusion and remands for prospective relief consistent with its opinion.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Affirmed and remanded, 5–4.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg.

**Opposing:** O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and Breyer.

The judgment invalidating the continued-registration exclusion is affirmed under contextual review. The mandate confines prospective relief to qualified parties' voluntary invitations and otherwise eligible voters, with implementation as stated in Part III.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns through the Tenth Circuit for a fitted prospective decree. Election officials must provide the lawful single-primary means of participation described in Part III. Any supported request for a narrower decree must establish a complete cure of the adjudicated common-policy violation.

### 2005-05-23 — [Deck v. Missouri; No. 04-5293.](../records/deck-v-missouri_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The Court reverses the sentencing judgment under review and remands for application of the constitutional harmless-error standard.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Scalia and Thomas.

The reviewed affirmance of the death sentence is reversed. Missouri must establish harmlessness beyond a reasonable doubt on the full penalty record; otherwise the death sentence must be vacated and the State must provide a lawful new penalty proceeding or a lawful noncapital sentence. The convictions remain intact.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns to the Missouri courts for full-record harmless-error review under the State's burden. If that showing fails, the State must provide lawful resentencing or the lawful noncapital alternative. At a new penalty proceeding the judge must determine actual security needs before exposing the jury to restraints and reconsider changed circumstances; the prior general rationale cannot substitute for that determination.

### 2005-05-23 — [Johanns v. Livestock Marketing Association; Nos. 03-1164 and 03-1165.](../records/johanns-v-livestock-marketing-association_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The Court vacates the reviewed First Amendment judgment and remands in both dockets. Five Justices direct rejection of the compulsory-financing claim on the governmental-message ground; Justice Ginsburg supports formal vacatur and remand on a different proposed commercial-justification ground.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No. 03-1164: reviewed constitutional judgment and dependent permanent collection injunction | Vacated and remanded, 6–3 as a formal disposition | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter | Ginsburg seeks a different commercial-application remand; the other five adopt the controlling instructions below |
| No. 03-1165: same reviewed judgment | Vacated and remanded, 6–3 as a formal disposition | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter | Same distinct grounds and remedial scopes as No. 03-1164 |
| Both dockets: governmental-message ruling and operative remand instructions | Compulsory-financing claim rejected and its dependent declaration and whole-checkoff restraint removed, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Remaining claims and independently supported relief retain their own proceedings |

The three Justices opposing complete vacatur would affirm protection against compulsory private advertising, vacate the categorical nonseverability determination, and retain collection protection during a genuine severability and remedy inquiry. Ginsburg would vacate the permanent decree but return the constitutional justification itself for application of her proposed commercial standard; she does not join the five-Justice mandate resolving this funding challenge for the Government.

**Procedure After Action.**

Both dockets return through the Eighth Circuit. The lower court must dissolve the declaration and collection restraints resting solely on the rejected compulsory-financing ground, and proceed with the actual remaining claims and independently supported relief. Any separately established attribution injury receives its own appropriate adjudication and remedy.

### 2005-05-23 — [Lingle v. Chevron U.S.A. Inc.; No. 04-163.](../records/lingle-v-chevron-usa-inc_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The Court reverses the judgment resting on the substantial-advancement taking theory and remands.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The lower court must remove the declaration or injunction resting solely on the rejected taking theory and enter judgment consistent with its rejection. Any independently preserved matter must satisfy its own substantive and procedural predicates.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns through the Ninth Circuit for removal of relief resting on the rejected test and entry of judgment rejecting Chevron's sole surviving efficacy claim. Hawaii may enforce the rent cap subject to other valid orders or independently preserved claims.

### 2005-05-23 — [Lofton v. Secretary of the Department of Children & Family Services; Eleventh Circuit, 358 F.3d 804.](../records/lofton-v-secretary-of-the-department-of-children-and-family-services_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The Court reverses the judgment sustaining the categorical exclusion and remands for declaratory and injunctive relief under the Equal Protection Clause.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Scalia and Thomas.

Florida must accept and evaluate applications without the categorical orientation bar in §63.042(3), under otherwise lawful child-specific eligibility and best-interest criteria. Relief corrects the statewide eligibility policy for all applicants governed by it unless Florida establishes that a narrower order fully cures the same defect. Existing placements and guardianship rights retain their present legal scope.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for a declaration and an injunction enforcing equal eligibility. The district court must establish and enforce the compliance schedule, including corrected instructions, notice to responsible officials and prompt lawful reconsideration within ordinary procedures. Actual applications remain subject to child-specific determinations, existing parental rights and lawful adoption requirements.

### 2005-05-23 — [Medellín v. Dretke; No. 04-5928.](../records/medellin-v-dretke_dismissal_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The writ of certiorari is dismissed as improvidently granted. The Court issues a per curiam explanation. The dismissal rests on the materially changed vehicle, not approval of the Fifth Circuit's substantive treaty reasoning. The Court does not publish an overall tally or a full disposition lineup; the separate writings state their authors' public positions.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

This grant terminates. The Fifth Circuit's certificate denial remains in place, and the separate Texas proceeding retains its pending status. Dismissal does not affirm the lower court's categorical treaty rationale. A later request for review must arise from an actual judgment and satisfy its own timing and jurisdictional requirements.

**Procedure After Action.**

The writ is dismissed. The parties continue the pending Texas proceeding under applicable law. Any later petition must identify its actual judgment and satisfy the applicable review requirements.

### 2005-05-31 — [Arthur Andersen LLP v. United States, No. 04-368.](../records/arthur-andersen-llp-v-united-states_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The judgment is reversed, the challenged conviction is set aside, and the case is remanded.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The conviction and its dependent sentence are set aside for prejudicial instructional error. Further proceedings must apply the correctly stated elements, with any independently preserved sufficiency issue and the ordinary double-jeopardy limits governing the relief then available. This trial-error disposition directs neither an acquittal nor a mandatory retrial.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for lawful further proceedings under the correct elements. The challenged conviction and dependent sentence cease to support punishment; ordinary sufficiency and double-jeopardy rules determine the proceedings available on remand.

### 2005-05-31 — [Cutter v. Wilkinson, No. 03-9877.](../records/cutter-v-wilkinson_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The judgment invalidating section 3 on its face under the Establishment Clause is reversed, and the case is remanded.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The statutory claims return for adjudication of coverage, actual burdens, application-specific defenses and appropriate claimant-specific relief, together with properly preserved distinct constitutional defenses.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns for ordinary adjudication of the inmates' statutory claims and preserved defenses. The courts must determine the actual substantial burdens, supported institutional justifications and lawful relief for claimants with a continuing interest, under the statutory and constitutional limits stated above.

### 2005-05-31 — [Tory v. Cochran, No. 03-1488.](../records/tory-v-cochran_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The unopposed motion to substitute Sylvia Dale Mason Cochran as respondent is granted. The California appellate judgment is vacated and the case is remanded.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

**Vacated and remanded, 7–2.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.
**Opposing:** Scalia and Thomas, who would dismiss the writ as improvidently granted.

The unsupported all-public-topic prohibition and enforcement dependent on it cannot continue unchanged. The lawful liability findings and independently valid unreviewed relief are preserved. An appropriate party may seek new relief in state court supported by a continuing claim and fitted to the changed circumstances; the mandate neither supplies a replacement decree nor establishes a successor's substantive entitlement.

**Procedure After Action.**

The case returns to the California courts for proceedings consistent with vacatur of the present topic prohibition and its dependent enforcement. Any request for new relief must rest on the requesting party's own entitlement, a supported continuing wrong and a precisely fitted decree. Lawful liability determinations and independently valid unreviewed relief remain operative.

### 2005-05-31 — [Tucson Woman's Clinic v. Eden; review of 379 F.3d 531, amended August 23, 2004.](../records/tucson-womans-clinic-v-eden_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Public Action.**

The amended judgment is affirmed in part and vacated in part, and the case is remanded for the access and independent patient-equality proceedings, precise remedial implementation and severability described below.

**Judgment & Remedy.**

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Reopening of the cumulative access claim | Affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Scalia and Thomas | Factual proceedings on supported prospective burdens, provider capacity, delay and necessary care |
| Complete six-step access instruction and additional mandate | Required, 6–3 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Kennedy, Scalia and Thomas | Apply the full framework, including remaining severe and meaningful interference; Kennedy supports only the narrower protected-choice, obstacle and necessary-care inquiry |
| Ordinary professional and volume classifications | Rejection of challenges affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Those categories survive the presented equal-protection objections |
| Independent patient sex-equality claim | Categorical dismissal vacated, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Determine actual classification or purposeful discrimination and apply the corresponding independent standard |
| Immediate compulsory clinic entry | Confined invalidation affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Restrain entry compelled without a warrant or adequate established exception; retain lawful consent, warrant and genuine-exigency routes |
| Broad department record access and copying | Privacy relief affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Scalia and Thomas | Confine relief to the established unjustified permissions and dependent enforcement |
| Identified ultrasound transfer and retention by APEX | Privacy relief affirmed, 7–2 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer | Scalia and Thomas | Restrain the established inadequately bounded contractor access and retention |
| Bounded professional-board serious-incident reports | Rejection of the privacy challenge affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Preserve the limited report and its distinct confidentiality protections |
| Undefined independent dignity sanctions | Confined invalidation affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Remove independent civil, criminal and adverse licensing consequences resting solely on the undefined duty |
| Facial arbitrary-private-delegation claim | Rejection affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Preserve the stated qualification requirement subject to the separate access proceedings |
| Severability and prompt clarification of the operative restraint | Required, 9–0 at the common independent-violation scope | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | — | Apply Arizona law to the precise defects and dependent consequences; promptly specify retained duties and the existing stipulated protection |

The stipulated interim restraint continues according to its terms pending a reasoned lower-court order under ordinary equity. The extent of further constitutional relief follows the component grounds above: six support the complete access mandate, seven the narrower protected-choice/necessary-care and substantive-privacy grounds, and all nine the common independent search and notice corrections. The lower court must identify the remaining lawful package and decide relief at the proved scope.

**Procedure After Action.**

The lower court must promptly identify the invalid commands and dependent enforcement, determine what lawful requirements remain under Arizona severability law, and specify the existing stipulated restraint's operation. The access and independent patient sex-equality claims proceed under the stated rules. Any further or modified protection must correspond to proved injury and scope and satisfy the applicable equitable conditions; the current stipulation remains governed by its terms until a reasoned further order.
