## Material published positions

- **Smith, November 15:** Scalia's dissent, joined by Thomas, would reconsider the constitutional entitlement to independent culpability mitigation beyond factual special issues; alternatively, it reads the specific mitigation supplement as an exception permitting a coherent negative answer. It supports affirmance, not the Court's effect remand.
- **Currency, November 15:** Kennedy's partial dissent, joined by O'Connor and Scalia, accepts the claimant's own interest and governing proportionality rules but finds full forfeiture permissible. The unreported amount, reporting-system function, deception and legislative sanctions make excessiveness unproved in their application; they neither assume another crime nor find the higher hypothetical fine bracket established.
- **Anderson, November 30:** Scalia's concurrence confines his polling-environs approach to historically administered environs during voting and finds this neighborhood reach unsupported. Thomas's separate historical and strict-scrutiny approach protects direct political communication without joining the Court's full forum method. Both join the financial and remedial grounds.
- **Koons, November 30:** Scalia's dissent reads the retained exception as clause-bound in the amended three-clause arrangement and would affirm the twice-finance-charge statutory amount. He disputes the general/specific reading, not independent liability or other remedies.
- **Roe, December 6:** Stone-Zsela's dissent would extend NTEU's citizen-expression reasoning to the particular outside publication and require ordinary Pickering adjudication of the actual police association and employment justification. Roe must establish protection and causation; the City may prove actual grounds and a lawful same-decision defense. The broad prospective-ban burden does not automatically govern the individual discharge.
- **Brosseau, December 13:** Stevens applies the existing defense and regards Garner as sufficiently clear on the assumed nonthreatening flight, leaving proper factual adjudication rather than ending the claim. Stone-Zsela separately proposes replacing broad legal-notice immunity with the official's preponderance proof of identified binding precedent or enactment directly authorizing the conduct, genuine reliance and reasonable applicability. Legal silence, departmental practice and lack of a factual twin would not suffice; a supervisor's instruction would qualify only by accurately conveying such authority actually relied on. That proposal receives no Associate join and is not current law. It retains the complete force merits and the existing cause of action, with absolute, sovereign and independent institutional immunities outside its scope.
- **Kowalski, December 13:** Ginsburg's dissent, joined by Stevens and Souter, treats the denied professional relationship, regular appointment practice, pleaded economic loss and the obstacle of asserting counsel rights without counsel as sufficiently direct for the third-party route. It would return only genuinely unresolved preserved matters, without inventing missing injury allegations or deciding the appointment merits.

- **2005-01-12 — [Clark v. Martinez](../records/clark-v-martinez_merits_2005-01-12.md#public-projection).** Thomas dissents. He would reconsider Zadvydas because the statute authorizes detention beyond the removal period without the implied general duration limit. Alternatively, he would preserve that decision's admitted-person scope and express reservation: the constitutional concerns supporting its construction do not apply identically to persons never admitted. He rejects using the earlier avoidance construction to resolve a materially different constitutional setting. He would reverse Martinez's statutory release entitlement and affirm Benitez's denial of this general duration claim, with implementation under the governing custody procedures rather than an automatic rearrest command.

- **2005-01-12 — [Jama v. Immigration and Customs Enforcement](../records/jama-v-immigration-and-customs-enforcement_merits_2005-01-12.md#public-projection).** Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer, dissents. Reading the destination provisions together, they understand the accepting-country description in (E)(vii) as identifying a shared feature of the connected-country routes. Otherwise officials could use (E) to send a person to the same nationality country whose lack of acceptance defeats (D), undermining the integrated scheme. They acknowledge that (C) permits disregard of the person's designation and does not command it whenever a listed condition occurs; their position is an acceptance requirement for the connected-country route presented here, not an absolute rule for every statutory step. United States National Bank of Oregon supports giving the enactment's whole structure priority over an isolated grammatical inference. They would reverse and preserve the acceptance-based bar to removal to Somalia under the asserted birthplace route.

- **2005-01-12 — [Nordyke v. King](../records/nordyke-v-king_merits_2005-01-12.md#public-projection).** Stevens, joined by Breyer, concurs in Parts I and III and in both judgments. Their Wright position accepts personal invocation, concrete examination of the burden and justification, and application of binding precedent. They continue to withhold assent from the abstract severity classifications and separately formulated home rule. Their objection concerns renewed doctrinal endorsement, not the lower court's duty to perform the required analysis on this record. Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in Parts I and III and in both judgments. Their Love and Wright positions support the personal right and an inquiry grounded in constitutional text and history. They retain objections to general severity tiers and the distinct home formulation while recognizing that lower courts must apply Wright as controlling precedent. County ownership does not erase the right, and the record requires actual application before relief.

- **2005-01-12 — [United States v. Booker](../records/united-states-v-booker_merits_2005-01-12.md#public-projection).** O'Connor, joined by Kennedy and Breyer, dissents from the constitutional holding. Their earlier Angle positions distinguish the statutory offense limits from rules regulating judicial selection within them. Mistretta's institutional sentencing arrangement and the established judicial sentencing role support reconsidering the extension of element protections to those internal constraints. They would permit the challenged judicial findings, reverse Booker's constitutional ruling and return Fanfan for ordinary mandatory-Guidelines sentencing under that preferred rule. They recognize that Angle governs unless reconsidered; they do not treat the federal question as previously unanswered. Breyer's separate Part I explains his additional constitutional position. He finds no coherent logical distinction between a fact raising a mandatory minimum and one raising a maximum, yet opposes extending the element requirement to the sentencing system in either form. That disagreement concerns the constitutional allocation itself rather than an exception consistent with Angle or Harris. In Part II, Breyer is joined by O'Connor, Kennedy and Ginsburg. Given the Court's constitutional holding, they would sever §3553(b)(1), §3742(e) and necessarily incompatible cross-references, preserving guided advisory sentencing. Congress's integrated arrangement relied on judicial assessment of actual conduct and sentencing information beyond charging choices; compulsory ranges rebuilt around jury findings would create disparities and unequal upward and downward operation. They view advisory guidance as the closer surviving statutory design. Courts would remain required to consider the Guidelines and policy statements, offense and offender circumstances, lawful alternatives, seriousness, respect for law, just punishment, deterrence, public protection, effective correctional needs, avoidance of unwarranted disparities and restitution. The sentence would have to be sufficient but no greater than necessary for the applicable statutory purposes, accompanied by reasons and reasonableness review. They would affirm the setting aside of Booker's sentence and resentence him under that system, but vacate Fanfan's sentence for advisory reconsideration even though he suffered no constitutional enhancement error. Ginsburg joins the Court's constitutional rule and this remedial position; the other three retain their constitutional objections.

- **2005-01-24 — [Bell v. Cone, No. 04-394](../records/bell-v-cone_merits_2005-01-24.md#public-projection).** **Stone-Zsela, concurring in Part I and in the judgment:** The omitted citation cannot establish constitutional failure, but the Chief would return the actual state merits treatment and narrowing application for the corrected inquiry rather than conclusively resolve them here. *Sochor* makes substantive legal operation decisive, while *Bell*, 535 U.S. 685, requires the proper statutory gateway and state-adjudication date. He would require the lower court to identify what the state court actually did, apply the dated law and decide any surviving jury-guidance and cure issue without presuming an omitted step. *Richmond*, *Lambrix* and *Brecht* require an actual authorized correction or the State's governing beyond-reasonable-doubt showing once a reviewable violation exists. If relief is warranted and neither route sustains the sentence, a conditional sentence writ must specify a reasonable correction period, require the State to report a lawful replacement determination and require removal of the death sentence if correction does not occur. He joins neither Part II's conclusive application nor its §2254(b)(2) rationale, and does not join Part III's closure of the narrowing inquiry. **Scalia, concurring in Parts I and II and dissenting in part:** The actual state explanation establishes constitutionally adequate judicial narrowing, so the reviewed aggravator claim should end. *Sochor* recognizes the narrowing work a state court may perform, and *Early* and *Visciotti* reject a citation or curative-label prerequisite where the substantive application is supported. Section 2254(b)(2) permits denial despite unresolved nonexhaustion. He objects that the further mandate keeps the reviewed ground open without an established distinct defect. He would reverse and finally deny that ground; a genuinely independent pending claim must establish its own procedural and constitutional basis. **Thomas, concurring in Parts I and II and dissenting in part:** The state opinion substantively applies the authoritative construction. Different formulations and an omitted citation do not establish an additional constitutional defect requiring continuation of this reviewed claim. His position preserves *Richmond*'s actual-cure requirement when an available weighing violation is established; the present narrowing determination does not establish that predicate. *Early*, *Visciotti* and *Esparza* support final denial on the actual claim and dated law, with §2254(b)(2) available despite nonexhaustion. He would reverse on this ground and return only independent remaining claims, without transferring the separate risk-factor harmlessness ruling to a different error.

- **2005-01-24 — [Illinois v. Caballes, No. 03-923](../records/illinois-v-caballes_merits_2005-01-24.md#public-projection).** **Souter, dissenting:** The dog was used to investigate concealed effects and to establish grounds for opening the trunk. Souter would treat that investigative examination as a search requiring an independent lawful basis beyond the speeding detention. *Place* supplies a special privacy distinction only when the method is genuinely confined to contraband; sufficient reliability for probable cause does not establish the absolute informational exemption asserted here. *Jacobsen* concerned a test of material already lawfully obtained, whereas this examination began an investigative process directed at a closed enclosure. *Kyllo* supports scrutiny of acquisition of concealed information, and *Edmond* requires authority for the actual intrusion. Souter accepts the individual reliability finding but rejects its use to settle constitutional classification. He would affirm suppression and also joins Ginsburg's separate seizure ground. **Ginsburg, dissenting, joined by Souter:** A traffic seizure is limited in scope as well as time. The compulsory canine investigation materially expands that seizure from traffic enforcement to a drug inquiry, even though the accepted duration remains lawful. *Robinette* preserves the limited authority conferred by the traffic encounter, *Edmond* distinguishes ordinary crime investigation from an independently justified seizure, and *Knowles* rejects treating a citation as general search authority. Ginsburg agrees that probable-cause reliability does not by itself establish an all-purpose contraband-only exemption, but her sufficient ground concerns the scope of this seizure. She would require articulable drug suspicion for the added investigation and affirm the Illinois judgment. Her agreement with the limited privacy objection is not a whole-opinion join in Souter's separate search rationale.

These published positions are noncontrolling except for the precise joins identified in the corresponding opinions. The Records distinguish binding rules from separate proposals and from agreement to apply an existing precedent.

- **2005-01-24 — [Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Taft; Sixth Circuit No. 01-4124; 353 F.3d 436](../records/womens-medical-v-taft_merits_2005-01-24.md#public-projection).** **Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part:** The Chief Justice joins the actual-elements rule, necessary-care holding, complete framework and implementing remedy. He would also vacate the conclusive statutory-coverage disposition. Voinovich makes clinically performed acts decisive, and the statutory D&E label supplies an answer only to the extent actual operation preserves the procedure used. He would require focused findings on that operation, usable alternatives and the affected patients instead of conclusively closing the coverage branch. He agrees that Stenberg's different definition cannot be imported and that neither a blanket method entitlement nor automatic invalidation follows. His disagreement concerns the additional coverage inquiry, not the health or full-framework remand he joins. **Kennedy, concurring in part and dissenting in part:** Kennedy joins the statutory disposition and the necessary-care vacatur and remedy. His Casey baseline position and Voinovich health joins preserve necessary life-and-health care, including grave medically established mental danger; his Stenberg position distinguishes supported comparative risk from an entitlement to a preferred technique. Ohio's new safeguards must receive their actual construction, but the conjunction and proper mental-danger theory require further application before conclusive validation. He would permit reasonable method regulation outside a denial of protected choice or necessary care and therefore opposes the additional command to justify residual meaningful interference under the full framework. His dissent is confined to that operative direction and its rationale; he supports the construction, clinical, severability and prompt interim-protection work required by the necessary-care holding. **Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas:** Both join the complete-elements interpretation and the rejection of categorical ordinary-D&E coverage. They would affirm the health disposition because they reject the constitutional abortion and necessary-health requirements imposed by Casey, Voinovich and Stenberg, continuing the grounds of their published separate opinions in those cases. Under their view, the defined intentional procedure may receive reasonable regulation for potential-life and medical-ethics purposes. Their disagreement concerns the governing constitutional rule; it does not turn conditional medical evidence into a finding that every substitute is equally safe. They acknowledge ordinary severability and remedial principles but oppose using the Court's constitutional premises to order health and residual-interference remands.

- **2005-02-22 — [Smith v. Massachusetts; No. 03-8661](../records/smith-v-massachusetts_merits_2005-02-22.md#public-projection).** **Breyer, dissenting, joined by Kennedy, Ginsburg.** The dissent would affirm the firearm conviction because the trial judge's midtrial ruling remained subject to Massachusetts's general common-law reconsideration power. It accepts Martin Linen's substantive acquittal inquiry, Fong Foo's protection despite legal error, and Price's rejection of a signature or jury-announcement prerequisite. Its disagreement concerns whether this proceeding had reached a completed acquittal at all. Rule 25(a) required an immediate ruling, but the dissent reads the state court's account of Haskell as permitting reconsideration of that partial ruling while the trial remained pending. It would not demand an additional rule specifically naming insufficiency determinations or an express reservation by the trial judge. The same jury remained empaneled, correction preceded closing arguments and instructions, and the prosecutor relied on evidence already presented. The Appeals Court reported no asserted change in defense strategy, and Smith did not request reopening after correction. In the dissent's view those features show a continued original adjudication with an opportunity to defend. The unqualified allowance, docket implementation and ensuing defense proceedings are the strongest contrary evidence. The dissent nevertheless distinguishes a conclusive statement of the judge's present assessment from an irrevocable disposition under the governing procedure. It reads Smalis as addressing further proceedings after appeal from a final acquittal. It does not permit reconsideration of an acknowledged completed acquittal merely because the ruling was wrong or no prejudice was shown; it finds the antecedent finality absent here.

- **2005-02-23 — [Dowhal v. SmithKline Beecham Consumer Healthcare; California Supreme Court No. S109306; 32 Cal. 4th 910](../records/dowhal-v-smithkline-beecham_merits_2005-02-23.md#public-projection).** Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter and Thomas, agrees that the initiative saving provision permits independently demonstrated conflict and that a true statement can materially mislead in context. The dissent would vacate and remand because the agency's rejection of identified formulations does not establish incompatibility with the entire state obligation, which permits explanatory language and several communication methods. Myrick and Medtronic require an operative source and precise comparison, and the Geier disagreement concerns whether that comparison proves exclusivity. The definitive response establishes more than mere approval, but its force cannot be enlarged into the missing claim-wide showing. Their proposed remand would preserve the operative federal decision while testing genuinely compatible compliance, rather than order a rejected warning. The distinction is the scope of displacement on this record, not a general prohibition on regulation of misleading labeling.

- **2005-02-23 — [Johnson v. California; No. 03-636](../records/johnson-v-california_merits_2005-02-23.md#public-projection).** Stevens agrees that the common standard governs and that personal immunity and prospective relief are distinct. He would hold the categorical policy invalid on this record because its racial proxy and repeated application to known transferees remain insufficiently justified even under Turner. Integrated activities and available individual information expose the inadequacy of the asserted blanket need. Adarand and Oneonta strengthen that conclusion. A changed review standard does not itself entitle the State to another merits opportunity. He distinguishes genuine emergency precautions and would remand only for personal defenses and appropriate relief, rather than enter an immediate damages award or an unsupported decree.

Thomas, joined by Scalia, would affirm under Turner. Confined reception cells, limited monitoring, changing gang affiliations and incomplete information make this temporary classification an operational security judgment reasonably related to protecting inmates and staff. General racial-classification decisions outside confinement do not silently displace the specific prison line. The integrated activities and existing information about transferees make the application closer but do not negate the particular risk in cells. Their argument does not validate racial allocation for its own sake or permanent system-wide segregation. If the Court imposes the common standard, they would permit genuine factual application rather than invalidate the policy immediately, and would apply the ordinary independent remedy restrictions. That alternative position does not change their primary affirmance vote.

- **2005-03-01 — [Cherokee Nation of Oklahoma v. Leavitt; Nos. 02-1472 and 03-853](../records/cherokee-nation-v-leavitt_merits_2005-03-01.md#public-projection).** 

- **2005-03-01 — [Petro-Hunt, L.L.C. v. United States; No. 04-190; judgment below, 365 F.3d 385](../records/petro-hunt-v-united-states_merits_2005-03-01.md#public-projection).** 

- **2005-03-01 — [Roper v. Simmons; No. 03-633](../records/roper-v-simmons_merits_2005-03-01.md#public-projection).** O'Connor accepts evolving standards and independent proportionality review, as she did in Atkins, but finds this extension insufficiently supported. The changes since Stanford are fewer and slower, continuing authorization remains substantial, and older adolescents vary in maturity. In her view, the evidence does not establish that individualized consideration cannot identify an offender sufficiently culpable for death. Atkins's distinct disability category and its reliable-status inquiry therefore do not decide this age question. She would preserve Stanford's eligibility rule, give youth its substantial mitigating force and return the case for lawful state proceedings. Her position does not reject independent proportionality judgment or treat legislatures as its exclusive source.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, objects both to the Court's method and to its account of domestic evidence. Stanford leaves the disputed eligibility rule in force unless the Constitution supplies a sound basis for change. The twelve States rejecting all capital punishment cannot be counted as twelve age-specific enactments; limited movement and continuing permission among capital States do not establish the claimed rejection. Rare use may reflect careful selection rather than repudiation, and grave planned crimes challenge a universal account of insufficient culpability. They oppose using independent judicial penal judgment to create the category and regard the showing as inadequate even within the prevailing evolving-standards inquiry. Atkins remains binding for its actual disability class, and youth must still receive its lawful mitigating consideration. They would reverse the federal categorical ruling and remand for lawful state proceedings, rather than direct execution.

- **2005-03-02 — [Catholic Charities of Sacramento, Inc. v. Superior Court; California Supreme Court No. S099822; 32 Cal. 4th 527](../records/catholic-charities-v-superior-court_jurisdiction_2005-03-02.md#public-projection).** Stevens would exercise jurisdiction under Cox's first category. Richards makes practical completion decisive even when the state order bears an interlocutory label. California rejected every presented constitutional theory on accepted organizational facts and conceded statutory ineligibility. No identified substantive issue remains whose resolution could alter the requested relief. In his view, denial of permanent relief is therefore preordained, and another adjudication would implement an already conclusive legal result.

His Jefferson dissent explains why repetition serving no useful finality purpose should not postpone review. Ritchie's practical approach reinforces that concern, although the first Cox category supplies the ground here. He distinguishes his joins in Florida v. Thomas and Johnson because those cases identified substantive proceedings capable of changing or eliminating the federal dispute. The majority's distinction between provisional entitlement and permanent rights gives insufficient effect to the exhaustive resolution of the legal claims in this record. His dissent concerns jurisdiction and expresses no disposition of the constitutional merits.

- **2005-03-02 — [Tenet v. Doe; No. 03-1395](../records/tenet-v-doe_merits_2005-03-02.md#public-projection).**


- **2005-03-07 — [Ballard v. Commissioner; No. 03-184; consolidated with Estate of Kanter v. Commissioner, No. 03-1034.](../records/ballard-v-commissioner_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection).** **Thomas, dissenting.** Thomas would affirm both judgments. The 1983 amendment deliberately removed automatic party service and changed filing to submission. In his view, the Tax Court reasonably reads the amended rule to permit a special trial judge to reconsider a report before the regular judge adopts the final endorsed version. The rule still requires deference to that judge's findings, but it does not expressly require disclosure of every earlier internal version.

Bowles v. Seminole Rock & Sand Co. supports respect for a reasonable interpretation of the institution's own rule. Freytag preserves the special trial judge's work and the regular judge's final authority; neither role proves that an initial report became immutable on submission. Full adoption of the special trial judge's endorsed findings can satisfy the required weight. Thomas also regards ordinary evidentiary adjudication by the authorized judge as adequate against the confined due-process objection absent a demonstrated hearing defect. He rejects treating an unproved account of internal revision as proof that the prescribed review failed. The Court answers that access is required to test the retained legal weight, regardless of whether misconduct or a changed tax result can yet be established.

- **2005-03-07 — [Shepard v. United States; No. 03-9168.](../records/shepard-v-united-states_merits_2005-03-07.md#public-projection).** **O'Connor, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Kennedy and Breyer.** The partial dissent would permit a confined inquiry into the plea's actual shared factual basis using reliable contemporaneous records. In its view, Taylor's examples of trial documents do not establish an exclusive list for pleas. Matching complaints, applications and reports, together with the State's factual-basis requirements, may reliably identify the offense both sides understood was admitted. Equivalent guilty pleas should not have different federal consequences solely because one State records a colloquy differently. The inquiry would remain directed to the conviction rather than an unrestricted retrial of old conduct.

The three Justices nevertheless agree that the unconditional enhancement command must be removed under the governing Gatewood and Booker decisions. Their earlier constitutional objections remain, but those objections do not permit the lower court to impose punishment through a procedure current law forbids. They join Part II's present-law remand and oppose only the categorical statutory restriction in Part I. The Court answers their statutory position by distinguishing reliable proof of conduct from a record establishing what the plea necessarily admitted.

- **2005-03-22 — [Black Political Task Force v. Galvin; lower-court No. 02-11190; 300 F. Supp. 2d 291.](../records/black-political-task-force-v-galvin_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection).** **O'Connor, joined by Kennedy, concurring in Parts I and II and dissenting from Part III and the judgment.** They accept the live appeal, governing section 2 coverage, supported causal findings and the Court's corrections to the legal test. De Grandy makes actual opportunity relevant without establishing either a quota or a safe harbor; Martinez requires applying that rule to the local proof. In their view, expressly excluding crossover opportunity from a principal proportionality comparison and treating the preliminary conditions presumptively could have affected the trial court's ultimate totality assessment. The concrete packing evidence could sustain liability, but the district court must weigh it under the corrected standard. They would vacate Count I liability and its dependent declaration, injunction and fee basis and remand for reassessment, preserving supported subsidiary findings. Plan C remains enacted, and any interim federal restraint must rest on ordinary equitable grounds. Neither apparent aggregate proportionality nor an additional feasible majority district dictates the outcome.

**Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurring in Part I and dissenting from the statutory holdings and judgment.** They would reverse and direct dismissal of Count I, with dissolution of its dependent federal injunction and fee basis. Their continuing construction, stated in their De Grandy, Valdespino and Martinez positions, confines section 2 to equal voting access, casting and counting rather than judicial allocation of electoral strength through district boundaries. They retain a results rule for practices within that coverage; the objection is not a demand for discriminatory intent. The statutory text, in their view, supplies no benchmark for the districting allocation required by the Court's dilution framework. If that framework is applied, the lower court's majority-only comparison and presumption furnish a further reason to reject its analysis, but that conditional objection is not a join in the reassessment mandate or the Court's findings of unequal opportunity. Reversal of the federal statutory judgment would leave enacted Plan C and dismissal of Counts II and III in place.

- **2005-03-22 — [Brown v. Payton; No. 03-1039; 544 U.S. 133.](../records/brown-v-payton_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection).** **Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Part II and the judgment; Stone-Zsela joins Part I of this separate opinion.** Part I finds both constitutional error and an objectively unreasonable state application. Skipper made relevant postcrime adjustment legally available, while Eddings distinguished exclusion from weight. The prosecutor repeatedly told this jury that the defense's principal mitigating case could not count. The court declined to correct that legal statement, and the instruction to consider all evidence was qualified by later directions that included factor (k)'s crime-centered wording. Defense advocacy was not authoritative legal authorization. The alternative attack on sincerity did not withdraw the exclusion claim. These features distinguish Boyde and, in the dissent's view, exceed the range of reasonable application rather than merely warrant a different independent answer.

Parts II and III, joined only by Stevens and Ginsburg, separately determine actual effect and would affirm sentence-only habeas relief. Under Brecht, O'Neal, Coleman and Penry II, the State must prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the preserved error did not contribute to the actual death sentence. The dissent considers the murder, sexual violence, other attacks, earlier violence and incriminating statements alongside the eight witnesses' conversion and conduct evidence and both sides' arguments. That aggravation could justify death in a lawful proceeding, but it does not establish what this jury did with its principal reason for choosing life after being told that reason was legally unavailable. Neither the admission of evidence nor the alternative weight argument establishes that the jurors instead weighed it. The State therefore fails its burden on the existing record. This is a separate actual-effect determination, not a structural-error rule or automatic relief from every Boyde violation. The dissent would preserve conditional sentence relief allowing a reasonable court-fixed opportunity for a constitutionally adequate penalty proceeding or a lawful noncapital sentence.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Part II and the judgment.** He joins Souter's Part I because the uncorrected legal exclusion of the only affirmative mitigation makes the state application objectively unreasonable and creates a reasonable likelihood that jurors treated the evidence as legally unavailable. The qualified general direction to consider evidence and the alternative prosecution argument about weight do not resolve that legal-use problem. He withholds Souter's completed harmlessness determination and proposed affirmance of final relief. He would affirm the statutory-gateway and constitutional-error determinations, vacate the final remedial component, and require a reasoned whole-record harmlessness adjudication with the State bearing the beyond-reasonable-doubt burden. The reviewing court must obtain and examine the full existing sentencing record and permit focused submissions; strong aggravation does not shift the burden or replace inquiry into the actual sentence. If the State fails, a conditional sentence writ must set and enforce a reasonable court-fixed period for a constitutionally adequate penalty proceeding or replacement of death with a lawful noncapital sentence. The convictions and unrelated habeas claims remain outside that relief.

- **2005-03-22 — [Muehler v. Mena; No. 03-1423.](../records/muehler-v-mena_merits_2005-03-22.md#public-projection).** Justice Kennedy, joined by Justices O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas, concurs in Parts I and III and dissents from Part II's continued-force disposition. They would hold the identified two-to-three-hour search-period restraint reasonable given the weapons search, four adults and one or two guards, even accepting the supported unfavorable factual account. Summers permits practical control during execution, and Graham does not require the best possible alternative staffing or restraint arrangement. Those circumstances, in their view, resolve this force ground for the officers.

Their conclusion carries concrete limits. Real pain or serious discomfort from prolonged cuffs requires officers to alter the conditions sufficiently to attend to the detainee's needs. Cuffs must be removed when an objectively reasonable officer would readily recognize that removal would neither compromise safety nor interfere with the search or cause substantial delay. Two to three hours calls for serious attention to continuing justification; it is not a categorical safe period. They join review of the separate post-search custody and damages issues, and the Court's present question-scope limit.

- **2005-03-29 — [City of Sherrill v. Oneida Indian Nation of New York; No. 03-855; the Lead, Eviction and Members cases at their reviewed scope.](../records/city-of-sherrill-v-oneida-indian-nation_merits_2005-03-29.md#public-projection).** **Stevens, concurring in Part II and dissenting from Part I; Stone-Zsela joins Part I of the dissent.** The two Justices would affirm effective protection of the identified Sherrill parcels against the challenged taxes and dependent enforcement. They require the Nation to establish the retained reservation and parcel connection and the City to identify a legally sufficient federal alteration or taxable disposal. The Treaty of Canandaigua recognizes the particular retained reservation and federal protection. Venetie's separate dependent-community criteria do not defeat that reservation status merely because the original reservation arose under state law.

The actual Buffalo Creek instrument supplies no completed Oneida surrender here. Articles 1 and 2 exchange Wisconsin interests for western lands; Article 3 conditions that new western interest, and Article 13 conditions Oneida removal on satisfactory arrangements for New York purchases. The separate Seneca and Tuscarora transactions cannot be attributed to the Oneidas. Existing recognition and succession likewise cannot be displaced by demographic dispersal alone. Yakima and Cass require the actual federal source, which this parcel chain lacks. Section 233 preserves existing tax protection; its distinct execution qualification for a tribal member's land-use or possession suit against another member is not a tax exception.

They reject the majority's independent qualification for the same reason. The Nation bought from willing sellers and resists a new governmental exaction on land it now owns. Effective prospective protection neither recovers ancient rents nor removes other private owners. Stevens's Oneida II concern about disruptive ancient recovery therefore does not decide this case. Equity may shape lawful relief, but reliance and elapsed time cannot create taxing power that federal law withholds. The majority's status formulation, in their view, permits the judicial extinction of protection while avoiding the federal act needed to remove it. Trust acquisition remains available; it is not a prerequisite to this existing parcel protection.

They would preserve the declaration and injunction and require ordinary implementation to identify the affected parcels, tax periods and enforcement measures. Sale or eviction consequences necessary to effective protection must be addressed according to their actual record status, independent legal requirements and legally required parties. That is bounded relief against these current assessments.

In Part II of his dissent, Stevens alone also objects to the Court's late formulation of the independent equitable bar. The majority regards that subsidiary ground as included in the tax-immunity question and its underlying substance as addressed by the competing arguments. Stone-Zsela's substantive position expressly reaches and rejects the equitable objection; he does not join Stevens's separate procedural objection.

- **2005-03-29 — [Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education; No. 02-1672; 544 U.S. 167.](../records/jackson-v-birmingham-board-of-education_merits_2005-03-29.md#public-projection).** **Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy, dissenting.** The dissent would affirm the categorical statutory dismissal. Section 1681 prohibits discrimination against a person on the basis of that person's sex. Intentional punishment for complaint activity is a distinct prohibition, even when the complaint alleges sex discrimination against someone else. Jackson's alleged loss of coaching duties and pay establishes personal injury; it does not establish that the enacted right protects the activity that allegedly caused that injury. The dissent's beneficiary and statutory-causation objections therefore remain distinct from standing.

Title VII's express retaliation provision illustrates that Congress can separately protect complaint activity. Sullivan construed section 1982's property-right protection, and neither its standing analysis nor its substantive setting compels the asserted Title IX funding obligation. Cannon supplies an action for rights Congress enacted; Franklin permits remedies after a statutory wrong is established. Neither decides this disputed expansion of coverage. Pennhurst adds a notice objection to imposing the asserted condition on recipients without a sufficiently clear statutory command. The dissent accepts the actual scopes of the existing Title IX and Title VI enforcement precedents but finds no enacted retaliation right here. It would conclude the appealed Title IX theory.


### 2005-03-30 — [California Public Employees’ Retirement System v. WorldCom, Inc.](../records/california-public-employees-retirement-system-v-worldcom-inc_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

KENNEDY, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SCALIA, SOUTER, THOMAS, GINSBURG, and BREYER, JJ., joined.

**Separate Writings.**




### 2005-03-30 — [Rhines v. Weber](../records/rhines-v-weber_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

O'CONNOR, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, SCALIA, KENNEDY, THOMAS, GINSBURG, and BREYER, JJ., joined in full. SOUTER, J., joined except as to requiring an initial showing of good cause and filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment, in which GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined. STONE-ZSELA, C. J., filed a concurring opinion. GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., also joined the Court’s opinion in full.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; Souter to the extent stated | Vacatur and remand | All propositions receive nine joins except the necessary initial good-cause condition, which receives eight |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Souter | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Would omit initial cause; accepts the remaining stay limits |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | — | Joins the Court | Ordinary civil proof of the predicate factual showing |

**Separate Writings.**

Justice Souter, joined by Justices Ginsburg and Breyer, would omit an initial good-cause requirement and focus on demonstrated intentional delay. Exhaustion can be difficult even for courts, and prisoners often lack legal training; a further threshold dispute may consume time without improving protection against abuse. He accepts retention power, the plainly-meritless screen, anti-abuse limits, reasonable deadlines and the judgment. Justices Ginsburg and Breyer share that practical preference while also accepting and joining the Court’s governing reasonable-explanation requirement.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins the Court and would expressly require the prisoner to establish the predicate factual showing by the ordinary civil standard. That showing concerns justification and the procedural conditions for retaining the case, with the merits inquiry remaining whether the claims are plainly meritless.


### 2005-03-30 — [Smith v. City of Jackson](../records/smith-v-city-of-jackson_merits_2005-03-30.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

STEVENS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and SOUTER, GINSBURG, and BREYER, JJ., joined in full. SCALIA, J., joined except for the independent judicial construction supporting recognition and filed a concurring opinion. STONE-ZSELA, C. J., joined in full and filed a concurring opinion. O'Connor filed an opinion concurring in the judgment, joined by Kennedy and Thomas.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia except as stated | Affirms on reasonable nonage factors | Six support operative recognition, particular-practice causation, reasonable-factors rule and application; five support independent judicial construction |
| Concurrence | Scalia | — | Joins the stated portions and the judgment | Recognition through the EEOC’s delegated interpretation |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | — | Joins the Court in full | Employer bears the reasonable-factors defense |
| Concurrence in the judgment | O'Connor | Kennedy and Thomas | Affirms because the impact cause is unavailable | No-impact construction and conditional specificity, reasonable-factors and proof views |

**Separate Writings.**

Justice Scalia concurs in the stated portions and the judgment. Congress gave the EEOC authority to implement the ADEA through §628, and its longstanding national interpretation recognizes restrictions on adverse-impact practices. He accepts the Court’s textual analysis as showing that interpretation is permissible, rather than as his independent judicial construction. The delegated national interpretation differs from the advisory response in Christensen and transaction-specific classification letters in Mead. Its recognition of impact liability and its use of business necessity are distinct propositions: the former is reasonable; the latter conflicts with the enacted reasonable-factors standard. He therefore joins the statutory justification rule and its application.

O'Connor, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, concurs in the judgment on the ground that the ADEA prohibits age-based employment decisions, not an additional unintentional-effects wrong. The effects words describe harmful forms of age discrimination; the reasonable-factors clause confirms a safe harbor for nonage criteria. The Act’s structure and the legitimate correlation of age with tenure and compensation counsel against transferring Griggs from Title VII. This writing also rejects binding reliance on the EEOC interpretation because it principally explains the exemption rather than construing the prohibitory subsection, and misstates the exemption through business necessity. Conditionally, if impact claims are recognized, these Justices accept particular-practice specificity and the narrower reasonable-factors justification; under their Wards Cove reading the employee must disprove the employer’s produced nonage explanation.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins the Court and places the statutory reasonable-factors defense on the employer. The employees must prove the particular practice, protected-age disadvantage and causal connection by the ordinary civil standard; the employer must establish its reasonable nonage justification. He concludes that the City establishes that defense on the reviewed plan facts.


### 2005-04-04 — [Consumers Energy Co. v. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission](../records/consumers-energy-v-ferc_merits_2005-04-04.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

GINSBURG, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which the other seven Associate Justices joined in full, and in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., joined as to Part I.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | All other Associate Justices in full; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela as to Part I | Affirms denial of review | Part I: comparability and review, all nine; Part II: rehearing prerequisite, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer |

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins the judgment and agrees that the separate Order No. 2000 independence-as-comparability objection is not reached; he does not join Part II's reasoning.

**Separate Writings.**




### 2005-04-04 — [Johnson v. United States](../records/johnson-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-04.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

SCALIA, J., delivered the opinion of the Court. All nine Justices joined Part I, recognizing the vacatur as a supporting fact. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Ginsburg joined Part II, adopting discovery diligence alone and reversal. SOUTER, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which O’CONNOR, THOMAS, and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg in full; O’Connor, Souter, Thomas and Breyer as to Part I | Reversal and remand | All nine join vacatur as fact; five join the discovery-only rule, application and remedy |
| Dissent | Souter | O’Connor, Thomas and Breyer | Would affirm | Procurement-and-discovery rule and its application; joins Part I |

**Separate Writings.**

Souter, joined by O’Connor, Thomas and Breyer, accepts the vacatur as a supporting fact but would require reasonable diligence in obtaining it after entry of the enhanced federal judgment, as well as diligence in learning of it. The federal judgment gives the prisoner a concrete reason to challenge the predicate. Because the prisoner initiates the process producing the new event, the dissent reads the express diligence condition to include reasonable pursuit; state collateral rules do not replace the federal sentence’s separate finality interest.

That rule includes three qualifications. First, delay in state proceedings not attributable to the prisoner after diligent initiation does not defeat the later trigger. Second, when the underlying grounds for the state challenge could not be discovered through diligence until after federal judgment, pursuit is measured from their discoverability, and diligent pursuit permits time to run from notice of the resulting vacatur. Third, even a prisoner whose pursuit was deficient may qualify if the federal motion was filed within one year of the notice date diligent pursuit would have produced.

The dissent would affirm here. Johnson waited from the November 29, 1994 federal judgment until February 6, 1998 to begin the state challenge. Even crediting all time before AEDPA’s April 24, 1996 enactment leaves more than twenty-one months. Limited education, self-representation and waiting for inmate assistance do not explain reasonable efforts or a specific impediment during that interval; the extension request did not pursue state vacatur. On that sequence the dissent concludes that diligent pursuit would have produced notice more than a year before the eventual federal motion, so the third qualification does not save it. Reasonable diligence permits genuine obstacles and does not demand the fastest conceivable action.
