### 2005-04-19 — [Monsanto Co. v. McFarling](../records/monsanto-co-v-mcfarling_merits_2005-04-19.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

SOUTER, J., delivered the opinion of the Court. All nine Justices joined Parts I, II and IV. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer joined Part III. SCALIA, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which O’CONNOR and THOMAS, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer in full; O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas as to Parts I, II and IV | Affirms in part, vacates in part and remands | Nine join patent/contract, general independent-tying rule and damages mandate; six join the antitrust application and remand |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O’Connor and Thomas | Would also affirm antitrust rejection | Joins common parts; rejects further antitrust inquiry on this presented reproduction-license theory |

**Separate Writings.**

Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas, joins the patent/contract ruling, the general rule preserving liability for genuine independent tying and the damages mandate. These Justices would also affirm rejection of the antitrust counterclaim on a confined alternative ground.

In their view, the presented transaction is an ungranted right to use patented second-generation seed while paying a technology fee instead of purchasing newly licensed seed. Refusal to offer that reproduction arrangement does not identify a distinct compelled acquisition as the condition of an otherwise authorized use. Their Kodak, CSU and Trinko analysis requires an independent competitive wrong beyond dependence on the protected product or a preference for different license terms. Reconsidering a product label does not cure that defect in the conduct actually asserted. They accordingly reject further inquiry on this claim while preserving the ordinary prohibition of an independently unlawful tie.


### 2005-04-26 — [Doe v. Porter; Sixth Circuit Nos. 02-5316 and 02-5823, 370 F.3d 558.](../records/doe-v-porter_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

BREYER, J., delivered the opinion of the Court. All other Justices join Parts I and II. Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg also join Parts III and IV; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgments in those parts.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | All eight other Justices in Parts I–II; Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg in Parts III–IV | Affirmance | I: family standing; II: governmental devotional instruction and injunction; III: protective order; IV: fees |
| Judgment-only agreement in Parts III–IV | Stone-Zsela | — | Affirms protective order and fee award | No separate opinion |

**Separate Writings.**



### 2005-04-26 — [Pasquantino v. United States, No. 03-725.](../records/pasquantino-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

Chief Justice STONE-ZSELA delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas. Ginsburg filed a dissent joined by Scalia, Souter and Breyer.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone-Zsela | Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Actual monetary property; domestic wire-fraud offense; revenue rule; scope and remedy |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Scalia, Souter, Breyer | Foreign fiscal claims and required congressional authorization |

**Separate Writings.**

**Ginsburg, joined by Scalia, Souter and Breyer, dissenting.** The general fraud language carries the established background limit on implementing foreign revenue claims. Although an accrued tax entitlement is monetary, that classification does not answer whether Congress authorized criminal punishment whose sole property object is the foreign fiscal demand. Domestic telephone calls supply the federal instrument but do not eliminate the need for clearer congressional authorization. Neder's treatment of inherited legal meaning and the statutory attention to foreign sovereign interests reflected in Empagran support that distinction. The four would reverse the convictions based on this theory, with their dependent sentences. Their shared ground concerns foreign fiscal claims, not a general exclusion of foreign private property.

### 2005-04-26 — [Small v. United States, No. 03-750.](../records/small-v-united-states_merits_2005-04-26.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

O'CONNOR, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Thomas dissented, joined by Scalia and Kennedy.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Domestic conviction category and reversal |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy | Inclusive statutory category |

**Separate Writings.**

**Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy, dissenting.** The unqualified phrase any court includes foreign courts, while Congress expressly confined neighboring exceptions to federal or state offenses. The dissent considers that contrast a reason to retain the broader predicate, even if the exceptions produce some asymmetry. The statute regulates later American firearm possession, not foreign conduct or foreign courts. Beecham's selector can operate by reference to the convicting jurisdiction, and Caron's restoration dispute supplies no missing geographical limitation. General concerns about foreign procedures cannot replace the enacted language. These Justices would affirm the statutory ruling; their position does not adopt the lower court's complete recognition test or decide a distinct constitutional objection to an individual predicate.

### 2005-04-27 — [Bates v. Dow Agrosciences LLC, No. 03-388; 544 U.S. 431.](../records/bates-v-dow-agrosciences-llc_merits_2005-04-27.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

STEVENS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join it in full; Scalia and Thomas join except Part III. Thomas filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Scalia.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer; Scalia and Thomas except Part III | Vacatur and remand with categorical protection for voluntary express warranties | I: actual duties; II: nonlabel conduct; III: voluntary warranties and corresponding DTPA; IV: parallel duties; V: registration, actual conflict and remand |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Thomas | Scalia | Supports vacatur and remand; opposes categorical warranty instruction | Requires actual federal comparison for label-based warranty and warranty-derived DTPA duties |

**Separate Writings.**

**Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** The separate opinion accepts the actual-duty rule, the exclusion of genuine nonlabel conduct, the protection of equal label obligations and the remand for fraud and DTPA comparison. Its disagreement concerns label-based express warranties and DTPA theories derived from them. A promise's voluntary origin does not answer whether state law imposes liability for label content that satisfies the federal obligation. Cipollone's recognition that common-law duties can impose requirements and Medtronic's distinction between equal and divergent conduct therefore call for the same federal comparison on those theories. A warranty-based duty may survive if it is genuinely parallel; the two Justices would require that determination instead of categorically excluding label promises from §136v(b). They recognize the pleading disclaimer and would give it its proper procedural effect while conducting the comparison the lower court omitted.

### 2005-04-27 — [Pace v. DiGuglielmo; No. 03-9627; 544 U.S. 408.](../records/pace-v-diguglielmo_merits_2005-04-27.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

SCALIA, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas. STEVENS, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which SOUTER, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined.

The Court's five-Justice coalition—Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas—joins Part I on statutory filing and Part II on assumed equitable authority and diligence. The dissent addresses statutory exclusion and does not reach independent equity.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Parts I and II: statutory filing and separate assumed-equity diligence |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Statutory tolling; independent equity not reached |

**Separate Writings.**

**Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, dissenting.** The state court conclusively determines whether state law permits relief, but that conclusion does not alone classify the application under the federal phrase properly filed. Artuz's distinction turns on how the rule operates. Governmental interference, previously unknown facts and later retroactive constitutional rights require judicial examination of the claims and their history. The dissent therefore treats this exception-bearing regime as comparable to a claim-relief limitation after an application reaches the proper tribunal in the required form.

The explicit filing command, separate statutory placement and concern about prolonging federal time do not, in the dissent's view, displace that functional inquiry. Genuine delivery, tribunal, form and fee requirements and actual pendency remain necessary. Carey addressed timely continuation through a gap in state review; it did not settle Artuz's reserved exception question for an application already lodged. Duncan's distinction between federal and state pendency remains intact. A contrary reading allows a later state timeliness ruling to consume the federal opportunity while the prisoner pursues exhaustion.

These Justices would exclude November 27, 1996 through July 29, 1999, remand for the exact federal calculation, and apply the remaining exhaustion, default and merits requirements. They accept the state untimeliness determination and reach no independent equitable-tolling conclusion because their statutory disposition makes that inquiry unnecessary.

### 2005-05-16 — [American Civil Liberties Union of Nevada v. Heller; Ninth Circuit No. 01-15462, 378 F.3d 979.](../records/american-civil-liberties-union-of-nevada-v-heller_merits_2005-05-16.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

KENNEDY, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer.

All nine Justices join Part I on standing and actual statutory reach. Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer join Parts II and III on the governing identification rule and the facial and concrete-application remand. Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, and Thomas separately filed opinions concurring in part and dissenting in part.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | All eight other Justices in Part I; Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Breyer in Parts II–III | Actual coverage; identification standard and open remand |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Stevens | Ginsburg | Joins Part I; existing text and record establish substantial overbreadth |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Thomas | — | Joins Part I; protected anonymous issue-pamphlet ground |

**Separate Writings.**

**Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** These Justices join Part I's standing and actual-coverage holdings. Stevens, joined by Ginsburg, accepts that Stengel and McConnell sustain a real field of paid electoral sponsor identification, including justified address information and some communications beyond express advocacy. The dissent nevertheless finds Nevada's actual coverage substantially excessive on the existing record. The law reaches neutral election information and ordinary cooperative leaflets throughout the calendar, so pooling a small expenditure exposes expression that an independently acting individual may publish anonymously. The State's offered source-tracing and information rationales do not justify that extensive reach merely because some candidate advertising may validly be identified. Correcting the appellate court's doctrinal description therefore does not require another facial merits inquiry. Stevens and Ginsburg would affirm facial invalidity and proceed to lawful severability and a conforming prospective decree, preserving valid attribution precedents and separate reporting duties.

**Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Thomas joins Part I's standing and actual-coverage holdings. Thomas relies on the historical protection of anonymous issue pamphlets developed in his McIntyre concurrence and maintained in his Stengel and McConnell positions. Ordinary issue expression retains that protection when people cooperate. Acceptance of external campaign reports and definite candidate-finance attribution does not justify compelled identification across Nevada's broad printed issue-information category. Because that protected reach is substantial and the offered express-advocacy rewriting cannot remove it, Thomas would affirm facial invalidity. His ground is distinct from adopting the complete modern overbreadth-balancing explanation; it permits genuine candidate-finance attribution and does not depend on a mandatory dollar floor.

### 2005-05-16 — [Granholm v. Heald and consolidated cases; Nos. 03-1116, 03-1120 and 03-1274; 544 U.S. 460.](../records/granholm-v-heald_merits_2005-05-16.md#public-projection)

**Published opinion relationship.**

GINSBURG, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer.

Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join the opinion of the Court in full: Part I construes the alcohol-specific constitutional and statutory authority; Part II addresses the discriminatory mechanisms; Part III applies the justification rule; Part IV resolves the docket-specific judgments and remedies. Thomas filed a dissent, joined by Stevens and O'Connor.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Parts I–IV in full; all three docket judgments |
| Dissent | Thomas | Stevens, O'Connor | Affirmative state authority over alcohol imports and distribution |

**Separate Writings.**

**Alcohol-specific dissent, shared by Stevens, O'Connor and Thomas.** Thomas, joined by Stevens and O'Connor, reads section 2's affirmative protection of state control over transportation and importation for local delivery or use, reinforced by the Webb-Kenyon Act's reference to shipments contrary to state law, as authorizing the challenged import and distribution conditions despite the negative Commerce Clause doctrine. The Wilson Act's equal-treatment language does not erase the additional authority the dissent finds in Webb-Kenyon and the Amendment. Young's Market directly addressed discriminatory import control and supports that reading.

The dissent regards local accountability and licensed distribution as within that affirmative authority. It distinguishes Bacchus's local tax preference from direct import-channel controls and, insofar as Bacchus's rationale necessarily forbids these arrangements, rejects that limiting interpretation. It would reverse the Michigan judgments and affirm New York on the Commerce Clause issue. Its ground retains Congress's affirmative regulatory authority and independent constitutional-right limits, including the distinct speech question. The dissent rejects application of the majority's nondiscriminatory-alternatives test to these authorized controls; it does not rest on a finding that the States have satisfied that test.

### 2005-05-23 — [Clingman v. Beaver; No. 04-37.](../records/clingman-v-beaver_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

STONE-ZSELA, C. J., delivered the opinion of the Court, unanimous as to Part I and joined by STEVENS, KENNEDY, SOUTER and GINSBURG, JJ., as to Parts II and III. O'CONNOR, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which SCALIA, THOMAS and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone-Zsela | All eight Associates in Part I; Stevens, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg in Parts II–III | Affirmance and prospective remand | I: protected association and contextual review; II: insufficient justification; III: implementing relief |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | O'Connor | Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Would reverse | Joins Part I; sustains the limited registration-choice condition |

**Separate Writings.**

Justice O'Connor, joined by Scalia, Thomas and Breyer, concurs in Part I and dissents from invalidation and its remedy. The writing accepts real reciprocal and overlapping association and applies Anderson and Burdick, but regards the available formal choice of nominating affiliation as a limited burden supported by identifiable parties, campaign planning, nomination-system operation and other-party continuity. Preventive regulation need not await a proved corrupted election. Timmons's protection of a consenting candidate designation does not, in their view, eliminate a distinct affiliation choice for selecting a party's nominee. They accept the lower factual findings and do not rely on the rejected confusion or raiding grounds. Their position depends on reasonable access to the registration choice presented here. They would reverse and sustain the challenged condition.

### 2005-05-23 — [Deck v. Missouri; No. 04-5293.](../records/deck-v-missouri_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

O'CONNOR, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, KENNEDY, SOUTER, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which SCALIA, J., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Parts I–III: individualized necessity, violation, Chapman burden and sentencing remedy |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Antecedent constitutional violation; would affirm |

**Separate Writings.**

Justice Thomas, joined by Scalia, dissents. The guilt-stage restraint cases protected the presumption of innocence, and the medication cases addressed distinct liberty and defense effects. A convicted capital defendant stands in a different posture. In the dissent's view, those authorities do not establish that visible restraints at punishment are constitutionally prejudicial without proof of actual unfairness or serious impairment of assistance in the defense. The writing acknowledges that chains can suggest danger but rejects treating that possible incremental effect as a sufficient constitutional violation on this showing. It would affirm. Its disagreement precedes harmlessness: it does not excuse an acknowledged constitutional error under a different Chapman burden.

### 2005-05-23 — [Johanns v. Livestock Marketing Association; Nos. 03-1164 and 03-1165.](../records/johanns-v-livestock-marketing-association_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

THOMAS, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and O'CONNOR, SCALIA and BREYER, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., filed a concurring opinion. GINSBURG, J., filed an opinion concurring in the formal vacatur and remand. SOUTER, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which STEVENS and KENNEDY, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Breyer | Government-ground vacatur and remand in both dockets | Parts I–IV: actual control, targeted financing, facial attribution, complete operative remedy |
| Concurrence | Thomas | — | Joins the Court completely | Distinct affirmative protection against objectively established involuntary personal attribution |
| Opinion concurring in the formal vacatur and remand | Ginsburg | — | Judgment-only agreement in that formal act; different remand work | Withholds governmental classification and resolved-claim mandate; proposes independent commercial justification and application below |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Souter | Stevens, Kennedy | Partial affirmance, partial vacatur and remedial remand; opposes the Court's complete vacatur | Accountability ground, current private-support rule, retained protection and actual severability inquiry |

**Separate Writings.**

**Justice Thomas, concurring.** He joins the Court in full and would also recognize a distinct constitutional injury when an unwanted message is objectively and involuntarily attributed to an identified individual or organization. Government control does not answer that personal injury. His position extends beyond the Court's reservation while respecting the absence of an established attribution predicate in this case.

**Justice Ginsburg, concurring in the formal vacatur and remand.** She withholds the governmental-message classification for this producer-presented campaign, without adopting every part of the dissent's affirmative accountability rule. Instead she would permit a commercial subsidy to be justified by a substantial governmental interest, direct advancement and reasonable fit. That approach expressly proposes modifying United Foods's independent-private-support rule; it does not treat economic regulation as outside the First Amendment. Her United Foods dissent supplies the proposed framework, and the consistent presentation of the commercial defense permits its consideration here. Industry welfare identifies the asserted interest, but does not establish all the test's predicates. She would vacate the permanent constitutional decree and return genuinely unresolved application to the actual bench-trial record, supplemented only as ordinary procedure requires, with the Government bearing the burden. Her proposal prescribes neither scientific or quantitative benchmarks nor an automatic new trial. Any interim protection would require ordinary equitable support. She therefore agrees in formal vacatur while withholding the Court's instruction that this constitutional funding question is resolved for the Government.

**Justice Souter, joined by Stevens and Kennedy, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Actual executive control is accepted, but hidden governmental selection does not provide the publicly identifiable responsibility needed to claim government speech for a campaign presented as the industry's message. Private drafting and targeted financing are not categorically forbidden; accountable governmental authorship must first exist. The writing expressly leaves open whether a discrete group's compelled support could raise another objection even when the Government openly identifies its speech.

Without the governmental justification, Abood, Keller and United Foods require an independently sufficient common function: advertising cannot justify its own compelled association. Producers retain their marketing autonomy and promotion is the principal object. Research spending does not by itself establish an adequate independent function. The preserved commercial defense fails as an independent legal cure even assuming effective promotion and sensible design; the objection is not an empirical finding that advertising failed. Glickman's qualifying collective program and Southworth's separate educational function remain distinct permissible relationships.

The writing would affirm protection against compelled advertising, vacate the categorical nonseverability determination and remand for actual statutory survival and remedial scope. The integrated collection restraint would continue until a conforming order permits a lawful, legally separable operation. Advertising's centrality and the deleted savings clause matter, but the deletion is not an affirmative legislative command resolving every remainder. The lower court would determine whether lawful operations can survive without rewriting the assessment. If they can, relief must permit them while fully protecting against the advertising defect; otherwise the whole-collection restraint may continue on that determination. The research allocation alone authorizes no fractional collection.

### 2005-05-23 — [Lingle v. Chevron U.S.A. Inc.; No. 04-163.](../records/lingle-v-chevron-usa-inc_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

STEVENS, J., delivered the unanimous opinion of the Court.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | All eight other Justices | Parts I–III: independent efficacy predicate, precise precedent change and surviving rules, claim-specific judgment |

**Separate Writings.**



### 2005-05-23 — [Lofton v. Secretary of the Department of Children & Family Services; Eleventh Circuit, 358 F.3d 804.](../records/lofton-v-secretary-of-the-department-of-children-and-family-services_merits_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

GINSBURG, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, KENNEDY, SOUTER, BREYER, JJ., joined. SCALIA, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which THOMAS, J., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Parts I–III: existing equality rule, categorical exclusion, fitted relief and alternative-claim nonreach |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Proposed rejection of intermediate scrutiny and separate rejection of the existing-family claim |

**Separate Writings.**

Justice Scalia, joined by Thomas, dissents on both claims. The writing acknowledges that Shahar makes intermediate scrutiny the current rule and expressly proposes rejecting that tier to apply its maintained ordinary-rationality position. Under that proposed standard, responsibility for permanent placements and the possibility that single heterosexual applicants may later form mother-and-father households sufficiently justify the distinction despite imperfect coverage. The dissent does not represent conceivable reasons as satisfying intermediate scrutiny.

On the independent family-liberty claim, the dissent distinguishes protection of an existing family's care and custody from a constitutional command to create adoptive status. Troxel protects a fit parent's established authority; Smith v. Organization of Foster Families requires attention to the statutory origins and expectations of foster relationships. The challenged law does not itself remove the children or dissolve current care arrangements. The biological father's consent in Houghton's case removes an obstacle but does not establish every legal adoption condition. Longstanding bonds and the practical financial and permanence interests are substantial, yet in the dissent's view they do not require conversion to a new status. It would affirm both claims on those grounds.

### 2005-05-23 — [Medellín v. Dretke; No. 04-5928.](../records/medellin-v-dretke_dismissal_2005-05-23.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

PER CURIAM. GINSBURG, J., filed an opinion concurring in the dismissal. O'CONNOR, J., filed a dissenting opinion. BREYER, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which STEVENS, J., joined. SOUTER, J., filed a dissenting opinion.

The per curiam explanation addresses the changed vehicle and the retained boundaries of certificate review. The concurrence and dissents state distinct preferences concerning retention and a limited certificate-review remand.

**Separate Writings.**

**Justice Ginsburg, concurring in the dismissal.** She would prefer retaining the submitted case through June 20, with a status submission by June 13 or promptly after an earlier state disposition, so that the Court could reassess the vehicle by a further actual order. That course would preserve review without awarding relief or waiving certificate requirements. With retention unavailable, she concurs in dismissal rather than reopening the older certificate proceeding. The proposed appellate remand retains the same unresolved gateways and the risk of parallel proceedings; its availability does not determine the pending state route. Her concurrence preserves the distinction between preferred case management and the procedural disposition she accepts.

**Justice O'Connor, dissenting.** She would vacate the certificate denial and remand for application of the actual certificate framework. The Fifth Circuit relied on a supposed categorical Supreme Court treaty bar that the confined Breard rulings did not establish. Slack permits correction of that distinct legal premise without issuing a certificate. The court of appeals must still enforce §2253(c), including the constitutional component, and all applicable habeas restrictions; it may again deny certification. Speculation about state relief or unresolved federal gates does not justify discarding the submitted controversy. She would permit, but does not require, the Fifth Circuit to await the Texas proceeding under ordinary case-management authority.

**Justice Breyer, joined by Stevens, dissenting.** They would first retain this case on the definite June 20 return and June 13 status terms described above. The pending Texas application may clarify the questions before the Court without establishing that they have disappeared. Slack and Miller-El preserve a real opportunity for review while keeping certification distinct from relief; comity supports coordination of actual domestic channels, not invention of a cause of action. If retention is unavailable, they would vacate the certificate denial for the limited corrective remand Justice O'Connor describes, expecting the Fifth Circuit prudently to accommodate the state proceeding. That expectation is not an independent command to hold, nor a direction to issue a certificate.

**Justice Souter, dissenting.** He likewise prefers the bounded retention and actual return order, drawing on the confined prior consular rulings and ordinary pending-case management. Rhines does not automatically prescribe a Supreme Court hold, but demonstrates the importance of proportionate coordination without surrendering lawful gateways. His fallback differs: he would vacate and remand only with a direction that the Fifth Circuit take no further substantive action while the Texas litigation runs its course. That condition preserves the new state route and avoids premature parallel adjudication. His remand would preserve all actual certificate and habeas requirements and would not confine the eventual permissible inquiry to an exhaustive list of treaty questions.

### 2005-05-31 — [Arthur Andersen LLP v. United States, No. 04-368.](../records/arthur-andersen-llp-v-united-states_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

SCALIA, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, KENNEDY, SOUTER, THOMAS, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports the judgment | the whole opinion; the complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer |

**Separate Writings.**



### 2005-05-31 — [Cutter v. Wilkinson, No. 03-9877.](../records/cutter-v-wilkinson_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

SOUTER, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SCALIA, KENNEDY, THOMAS, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., filed a concurring opinion.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports the judgment | the whole opinion; the complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Joins the Court's opinion and judgment in full | Additional explanation distinguishing federal legislation concerning religion from establishment and from enumerated-power authority |

**Separate Writings.**

**Thomas, concurring.** Thomas joins the opinion of the Court in full and adds that federal legislation concerning religion is not necessarily legislation establishing religion. A general accommodation provision does not necessarily compel a State to impose religious observance or support, or to prefer a sect. A categorical assertion that religion is exclusively a state subject therefore does not establish this facial violation. That distinction does not supply unlimited congressional power: Spending, Commerce and other unadjudicated power objections retain their separate questions. His additional ground is consistent with the controlling accommodation analysis and changes neither the statutory standard nor the remand.

### 2005-05-31 — [Tory v. Cochran, No. 03-1488.](../records/tory-v-cochran_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

BREYER, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, KENNEDY, SOUTER and GINSBURG, JJ., joined. STONE-ZSELA, C. J., filed a concurring opinion. THOMAS, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which SCALIA, J., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Ginsburg | Supports the judgment | the continuing controversy, present overbreadth after death and corresponding remedy; the complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer |
| Concurrence | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Joins the Court's common grounds and judgment; states an additional ground | The blanket topic prohibition also exceeds the established unlawful conduct independently of death |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would dismiss the writ as improvidently granted | Changed vehicle, available state correction and constitutional avoidance; no affirmative mootness or merits ruling |

**Separate Writings.**

**Stone-Zsela, concurring.** The Chief Justice joins the Court's continuing-controversy, present-overbreadth and remedial grounds. He further concludes that the decree's blanket topic prohibition violates the First Amendment independently of Cochran's death. Findings of coercive defamation justify an appropriate remedy for proved unlawful conduct, but they do not establish that every future public statement about Cochran or his firm is actionable. The command suppresses truthful criticism, discussion of this lawsuit and other protected expression without differentiating those statements from the adjudicated wrong. Madsen and Schenck require a supported remedial connection, while Alexander confirms the significance of an express future-speech ban; the content-neutral standard in the former decisions is not itself the standard for this content-based order. Death further weakens the stated objective of preventing Cochran's personal coercion. An interest in the practice does not supply findings justifying the decree's entire breadth. He would remove that public-topic prohibition, including dependent enforcement, while preserving lawful liability findings and independently valid relief. A different injunction confined to established unlawful conduct requires a concrete order and continuing remedial record; his ground does not decide every injunction following adjudicated defamation or supply immunity for threats, coercion or actionable false statements.

**Thomas, dissenting, joined by Scalia.** These Justices would dismiss the writ as improvidently granted. Cochran's death removed the original vehicle for the broad question on which review was granted. The new need to interpret, modify or dissolve the decree can be addressed in California, where constitutional objections remain available. A case-specific need to correct the existing command does not, in their view, restore the reason for retaining this changed vehicle or justify reaching a new constitutional issue. Their position neither affirms the decree's constitutionality nor decides the contested mootness question; it leaves the parties to seek appropriate state-court correction.

### 2005-05-31 — [Tucson Woman's Clinic v. Eden; review of 379 F.3d 531, amended August 23, 2004.](../records/tucson-womans-clinic-v-eden_merits_2005-05-31.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

GINSBURG, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SOUTER and BREYER, JJ., joined. KENNEDY, SCALIA and THOMAS, JJ., joined the portions stated below. KENNEDY, J., filed an opinion concurring in part. SCALIA, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part, in which THOMAS, J., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter and Breyer | Supports every component of the Court's disposition | The complete-opinion coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer |
| Partial join in the Court's opinion | Kennedy | — | Supports access proceedings on narrower grounds and every independent component | Joins the protected-choice, substantial-obstacle and necessary-care grounds; equality, entry, all information grounds, notice, qualifications and corresponding remedies; withholds both residual access tiers and their additional mandate |
| Partial joins in the Court's opinion | Scalia and Thomas | — | Support the identified independent dispositions; oppose access remand and department/APEX privacy relief | Join equality under Klinger, entry under Burger and protected-premises principles, the bounded board-report result and permission, confined dignity-sanction rule, qualification ruling, and ordinary severability/interim implementation for supported defects; withhold the substantive-privacy rationale and abortion-framework grounds |
| Concurrence in part | Kennedy | No other Justice | Explains narrower access agreement and withheld additional mandate | Protected choice, substantial obstacle and necessary care, with full independent-claim joins |
| Opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part | Scalia | Thomas | Opposes access remand and department/APEX privacy invalidation; joins the stated independent portions | Constitutional-source objections and their distinct dispositions, with common search, equality, notice, qualification and bounded reporting grounds preserved |

**Separate Writings.**

**Kennedy, concurring in part.** Kennedy agrees that supported predictions of lost provider capacity, delay and clinical risk require access proceedings concerning protected choice, a substantial practical obstacle and timely necessary care. His Casey, Lawall, Greenville and Taft positions support those protections, including competent individualized evidence of grave mental danger and serious health risk. He would permit reasonable safety regulation outside those boundaries and withholds the additional command to justify every remaining severe or meaningful interference under the complete residual framework. He joins the independent equality, search, recipient-specific privacy, board-report, notice and private-control holdings and effective remedies on the grounds he accepts. His disagreement with the residual tiers does not narrow those separate joins or oppose a necessary-care remand.

**Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas.** These Justices would sustain Arizona on the constitutional-abortion theory and on the substantive informational-privacy challenges to department and APEX access. Their current Taft position rejects the constitutional abortion and necessary-health predicates and would permit reasonable safety, potential-life and medical-ethics regulation. Their Lawall and Greenville positions likewise reject the asserted substantive source for the privacy framework. They do not rest on a finding that the providers' forecasts are false or that Arizona has adequate recipient and retention controls; the actual Arizona deficiencies differ from Greenville's safeguards.

They join the independent Klinger classification inquiry and patient-equality remand; the traditional protected-premises and Burger grounds against unsupported compulsory entry; the bounded board-report result; the precise dignity-sanction ruling; and rejection of the present uncontrolled-delegation claim. Their prior acceptance of a recognizable clinical best-practices core does not supply an ascertainable criterion for this abstract independent dignity punishment. They also join ordinary severability, removal of dependent consequences and prompt clarification of protection for the independent violations they accept, while opposing an injunction based solely on the abortion and substantive-privacy theories they reject.

### 2005-06-06 — [Gonzales v. Raich, No. 03-1454.](../records/gonzales-v-raich_merits_2005-06-06.md#public-projection)

**Opinion Topology.**

KENNEDY, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which STONE-ZSELA, C. J., and STEVENS, O'CONNOR, SCALIA, SOUTER, THOMAS, GINSBURG and BREYER, JJ., joined.

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
