| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports the judgment | the whole opinion; the complete coalition is Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer |

**Separate Writings.**

### 2005-06-06 — [Alaska v. United States; No. 128, Original.](../records/alaska-v-united-states_exceptions_2005-06-06.md#public-projection)

Scalia's partial dissent, joined by Thomas, maintains that section 6(e)'s reservation proviso is confined to the initial transfer's named statutory class and cannot establish retention of this Antiquities Act monument against section 6(m). It distinguishes the prior Alaska decision's assumed initial-clause coverage. This is a published noncontrolling statutory position; both Justices continue to accept the separate inclusion/retention framework and the Archipelago disposition.

### 2005-06-06 — [Spector v. Norwegian Cruise Line Ltd.; No. 03-1388.](../records/spector-v-norwegian-cruise-line-ltd_merits_2005-06-06.md#public-projection)

**Part III-B, supported by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter.** These Justices permit consideration of either the applicable statutory qualification or the maritime limit first when that resolves the particular claim. They distinguish Clark's common detention provision from duty-specific maritime interference. Thomas rejects both the sequencing discretion and their reconciliation with Clark; the discussion therefore supplies no controlling sequence or new Clark rule.

**Stone-Zsela's concurrence.** Once a challenged charge or policy is proved unlawful and prospective relief is properly before the court, the remedy should govern that policy and those it affects unless the operator establishes that a narrower order fully cures the violation. The district court should identify the proved practice and set an enforceable compliance measure within the applicable cause of action. That proposed allocation of remedial reach does not itself decide liability or specify construction work.

**Ginsburg, joined by Breyer.** The statute's actual international-conflict and safety qualifications adequately protect foreign sovereign interests in this passenger-service dispute. Once those conflicts are avoided, they see no additional internal-affairs exemption. They therefore join coverage, the statutory analysis and complete remand, but withhold assent from a separate exclusion based on permanent structural effects. Their conditional acceptance of an application-specific clear-statement method does not endorse the exclusion itself.

**Thomas.** A duty-by-duty inquiry permits nonstructural passenger claims, but structural obligations intrude on internal affairs without the required clear statement. He would retain dismissal of those claims. He rejects both the Court's expansion of readily-achievable analysis to actual international conflict and the four Justices' discretionary sequencing. Possible international discord is sufficient for his structural rule. His continued disagreement with Clark's uniform-limitation approach supports application-specific treatment, not complete remand.

**Scalia, joined by O'Connor.** Title III's integrated regulation of shipboard services and design requires a clear congressional instruction before application to foreign vessels. Potential international conflict and structural interference trigger that protection without proof of an actual treaty violation. They would therefore affirm dismissal of all present shipboard claims. Thomas joins the structural premise but rejects their extension of it to every nonstructural passenger duty.

### 2005-06-06 — [United States v. Kincade; review of 379 F.3d 813, Ninth Circuit en banc.](../records/united-states-v-kincade_merits_2005-06-06.md#public-projection)

Kennedy, joined by Thomas and Breyer, would recognize a defined identification exception for lawfully supervised offenders selected by objective conviction criteria. The continuing correctional relationship, ordinary medical draw, restricted identification profile and authorized-use controls make this program reasonable in their view. They maintain the position taken in their Rise partial dissent and would limit its contrary sufficiency rule in this setting. They acknowledge that Knights did not decide the suspicionless question; its consideration of a lawful supervision relationship supports their proposed extension, not an existing categorical authorization. Their exception depends on the actual collection and use limits rather than a general forfeiture of privacy.

### 2005-06-13 — [Bradshaw v. Stumpf, No. 04-637; 545 U.S. 175.](../records/bradshaw-v-stumpf_merits_2005-06-13.md#public-projection)

Souter, joined by Ginsburg, concurs. He identifies a fundamental-fairness problem if the State continues to defend death for Stumpf by asserting that he alone was the shooter after relying on Wesley’s sole responsibility to seek death there. His concern rests on the prosecutor’s duty to justice and the reliability required for capital selection, without depending on a charge of deliberate deception. The sentencing record must establish the conduct, constitutional significance, prejudice and appropriate correction.

Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurs. Newly obtained evidence and a later prosecution theory do not, in his view, by themselves undermine the fairness or reliability of the earlier proceeding. He stresses the adversarial opportunity to test conflicting evidence and distinguishes a newly discovered-evidence contention from an established constitutional violation. His agreement with the remand preserves that objection to the broader fairness theory.

### 2005-06-13 — [Grable & Sons Metal Products, Inc. v. Darue Engineering & Manufacturing, No. 04-603; 545 U.S. 308.](../records/grable-and-sons-v-darue-engineering_merits_2005-06-13.md#public-projection)

Thomas’s unjoined concurrence accepts faithful application of retained precedent but would consider whether section 1331’s original meaning supports return to American Well Works’ clearer cause-of-action rule. He contrasts its administrability and coverage of most federal litigation with uncertainty in Smith’s embedded-issue category. This is a noncontrolling proposal for a properly presented future case, not a limit on his present join.

### 2005-06-13 — [Johnson v. California, No. 04-6964; 545 U.S. 162.](../records/johnson-v-california-batson_merits_2005-06-13.md#public-projection)

Thomas’s dissent, unjoined, would allow California’s stronger first-stage showing as a permissible implementation of the constitutional prohibition. He distinguishes the prohibition on intentional discrimination from its burden-shifting machinery, drawing on Batson, Pennsylvania v. Finley and Smith v. Robbins for state procedural latitude. This is a dissent on the production threshold, not an affirmative race-neutral finding.

Breyer’s concurrence, unjoined, adopts the concern developed in his separate Miller-El writing of the same date: peremptory challenges can conceal pretext and stereotypes despite Batson’s protections, warranting reconsideration of their continued use. He fully joins the present decision. The companion writing is a published separate position, not earlier entering law.

### 2005-06-13 — [Miller-El v. Dretke, No. 03-9659; 545 U.S. 231.](../records/miller-el-v-dretke_merits_2005-06-13.md#public-projection)

Thomas’s dissent, joined by Scalia, evaluates the combination of offered reasons—rehabilitation and future dangerousness, death-penalty answers, relatives’ criminal histories, remaining challenges and relevant comparator differences—and concludes that the statutory burdens are unmet. They read Warren’s offered inconsistency more broadly and Fields’s assurances as compatible with a sincere concern. Oral questioning can track capital-punishment views; incomplete shuffle comparisons and office practices without a demonstrated prosecutor-specific link do not supply the decisive proof for them. Trial-court credibility and the presumption remain meaningful despite a delayed hearing. Their objection is to the lawful-record merits ground actually adopted, not to outside evidence the Court excludes.

Breyer’s unjoined concurrence joins the Court completely while proposing reconsideration of peremptories. The preliminary threshold, easily supplied neutral explanation and subjective-purpose inquiry may fail to uncover pretext or unconscious stereotypes. He weighs peremptories’ traditional role and litigant reassurance against excluded citizens’ equality and jury participation, draws on Marshall’s Batson concurrence, Goldberg’s Swain dissent, Edmonson, Stilson, Ross, Powers and the actual McCollum and J.E.B. decisions, and points to experience without peremptories as a reason for practical reconsideration. His noncontrolling reform proposal changes no current entitlement or remedy. Johnson’s same-date concurrence adopts this concern without treating the companion as prior law.

### 2005-06-20 — [American Trucking Associations, Inc. v. Michigan Public Service Commission, No. 03-1230](../records/american-trucking-associations-v-michigan-public-service-commission_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**Scalia, concurring in the judgment.** Scalia applies the approach reflected in West Lynn Creamery and his prior published Commerce Clause positions: a state measure is invalid under the negative Commerce Clause if it facially discriminates against interstate commerce or is indistinguishable from a type of measure the Court previously invalidated. Michigan’s fee meets neither condition. Scheiner charged interstate trucks even without local hauling, a material difference. This ground supports affirmance without joining Pike balancing, the full Complete Auto framework, or an independent internal-consistency rationale.

**Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** Thomas follows his published Hillside Dairy position that the negative Commerce Clause lacks a constitutional foundation adequate to invalidate state legislation. He would affirm for that reason. His objection reaches the doctrine itself and does not adopt Scalia’s separate precedent-based test.

### 2005-06-20 — [Dodd v. United States, No. 04-5286](../records/dodd-v-united-states_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, dissenting.** The dissent would read the new-right and retroactivity conditions together so that the special year begins when both are satisfied. A period that predictably can be consumed before the collateral qualification exists frustrates the opportunity Congress supplied. The completed-condition reading is textually permissible because the subordinate clause describes the qualifying right and can identify the point at which the provision becomes usable. The ordinary concern for a complete claim and the statutory structure support that construction.

Tyler’s multiple-holdings route can establish retroactivity and has been applied to the confined Cage rule, but it does not ensure prompt qualification of every newly recognized right. The dissent’s concern remains the short year expiring before a qualifying collateral route is available, particularly in combination with successive-motion authorization. It would reverse the timing dismissal and remand for any remaining qualification and merits questions. The shared ground rests on the assumed qualification in this case and does not resolve which court may make the retroactivity determination.

### 2005-06-20 — [Graham County Soil & Water Conservation District v. United States ex rel. Wilson, No. 04-169](../records/graham-county-soil-water-conservation-district-v-united-states-ex-rel-wilson_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**Stevens, concurring in the judgment.** Stevens regards the broad action-under-§3730 language as naturally inclusive of retaliation and recognizes the value of federal uniformity. The decisive contrary consideration is that the fraud-based trigger could exhaust the entire period before an actionable retaliation claim exists. He treats the ordinary accrual principle as sufficient to reject that result without requiring another feature first to establish textual ambiguity. He therefore supports state borrowing and remand without joining the Court’s principal interpretive reasoning in full.

**Breyer, joined by Ginsburg, dissenting.** The dissent would apply six years from the alleged or suspected §3729 violation. Retaliation is an action under §3730, and limitations provisions routinely measure time from alleged events without making ultimate proof of that event a condition of timeliness. This reading preserves independent retaliation liability; it does not require a successful fraud claim or move the federal starting date to the retaliation itself.

The government-burden provision’s own subject, the United States, limits its operation, while Congress expressly named subsections (a) and (b) elsewhere when it wished to narrow coverage. Congress could reasonably select one long period for related fraud and retaliation litigation. That clock can start before retaliation and could expire first, but the dissent considers the resulting risk less consequential than the short, varied periods produced by borrowing. Donnelley’s discussion of the difficulties of varied state periods reinforces the practical value of a uniform period. The dissent would affirm the Fourth Circuit’s six-year ruling.

### 2005-06-20 — [Rompilla v. Beard, No. 04-5462](../records/rompilla-v-beard_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent would affirm because §2254(d)(1) does not authorize disturbing Pennsylvania’s performance adjudication. Strickland leaves room for informed professional judgments about how to allocate limited investigative effort, and Visciotti requires more than disagreement with a state court’s application. Counsel conducted client and family interviews, obtained three examinations, and acquired the aggravating testimony. Those efforts and the limited information they produced permit a reasonable state appraisal even if additional file review would have been preferable.

The prosecution’s announced source is a substantial reason for further work, but the dissent does not regard it as establishing that every reasonable application of Strickland had to condemn counsel’s stopping point. Wiggins involved serious mitigation leads already exposed by the materials counsel possessed; the dissent distinguishes this investigation and warns that reliance on later discoveries can obscure the contemporaneous inquiry. The opinion does not assume that counsel examined the entire file. Its sufficient ground is the statutory limitation, so it does not independently resolve actual deficiency or cumulative prejudice. On that route it would leave the conviction and sentence intact.

### 2005-06-20 — [San Remo Hotel, L.P. v. City and County of San Francisco, No. 04-340](../records/san-remo-hotel-v-city-and-county-of-san-francisco_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**O’Connor, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, concurring.** The concurrence joins the full statutory holding and agrees that the existing state judgment must receive the recognition §1738 commands. It would reconsider Williamson County’s separate state-litigation prerequisite in an appropriate case. That requirement differs from obtaining a final administrative decision identifying how a regulation applies to the property. Once the government has made that decision, the concurrence questions the constitutional or prudential basis for requiring state compensation litigation before a federal takings action.

State courts’ competence to adjudicate federal rights does not explain why takings claims must uniquely begin there. Fair Assessment’s treatment of state taxation rests on a historically grounded comity principle and does not by itself establish a parallel land-use rule. Mandatory state litigation combined with ordinary preclusion can prevent a later federal district-court merits decision. That practical effect calls for examination of the prerequisite, not suspension of §1738. Neither party seeks its reconsideration here, the grant does not include it, and reconsideration would not benefit these petitioners.

### 2005-06-20 — [Mid-Con Freight Systems, Inc. v. Michigan Public Service Commission, No. 03-1234.](../records/mid-con-freight-systems-v-michigan-public-service-commission_merits_2005-06-20.md#public-projection)

**Kennedy, joined by O'Connor, dissenting.** The dissent reads subsection (b) to prohibit additional registration obligations imposed because a carrier conducts interstate transportation, while preserving general neutral registration that only incidentally covers interstate carriers. Subsection (a) defines the permitted federal standards, rather than every additional demand subsection (b) forbids. On that reading, a State cannot avoid the ceiling by asking for different information or spending the fee on safety administration. The information, decal and payment constitute registration even though they serve a regulatory purpose.

The Court's defined-subject construction, in the dissent's view, adds an exemption the excess-obligation sentence does not contain. Applicable subsection (c) commands still independently protect the specified federal demands; the general-neutral qualification does not permit excess covered fees. Yellow's enforcement of the federal benchmark leaves this scope question open.

The dissent would vacate and remand rather than invalidate the fee outright. Michigan argues that sections 478.2(1) and (2) together impose one neutral $100 requirement on Michigan-plated for-hire vehicles; the carriers dispute that account. The lower court's regulatory-purpose rationale did not resolve that characterization under the dissent's rule. The required inquiry concerns the combined scheme's operation, rather than an assumed condition linking the $100 payment to federal registration.

### 2005-06-23 — [Exxon Mobil Corp. v. Allapattah Services, Inc., No. 04-70; del Rosario Ortega v. Star-Kist Foods, Inc., No. 04-79.](../records/exxon-mobil-v-allapattah-services_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer, dissenting.** Section 1367 begins with an action already within original jurisdiction. The dissent reads that phrase to incorporate section 1332's settled amount requirements, including Zahn for class members and Clark for joined plaintiffs. Citizenship and amount are both statutory prerequisites; neither may be dispensed with merely because another claim independently qualifies.

The additional-party sentence still performs substantial work under this reading by changing Finley's restriction once an adequate original action exists. Subsection (b) prevents specified attempts to circumvent diversity limits through supplementary additions. Its omission of Rule 23 members and Rule 20 plaintiffs does not itself change the antecedent meaning of original jurisdiction. The dissent acknowledges the force of the broad grant and the omitted categories, but reads the statute as a whole against the established jurisdictional background. It would exclude the smaller dealer claims and sustain exclusion of the relatives' claims.

**Stevens, joined by Breyer, dissenting.** Reliable legislative evidence may corroborate the contextual reading even when the opposing interpretation claims textual clarity. The relevant House report described restoration of pre-Finley supplemental practice and preservation of diversity-only class-action requirements. An earlier study's discussion of changing Zahn does not establish that Congress enacted that different policy. This explanation supports the common dissent's interpretation; it treats legislative material as evidence of meaning rather than an independently enacted jurisdictional rule.

### 2005-06-23 — [Gonzalez v. Crosby, No. 04-6432; 545 U.S. 524.](../records/gonzalez-v-crosby_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Justice Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Souter.** They join the Court's classification and its independent Rule 60 requirements. They would vacate the denial and remand for ordinary discretionary assessment. Abdur'Rahman's separation of classification from entitlement requires attention to the procedural dismissal, the corrective legal development, actual review efforts, the possible merit of the underlying claim and the State's finality interests. The absence of any federal merits adjudication gives the inquiry particular weight.

Gonzalez's alleged attempted rehearing and the information accompanying the returned paper may explain the lost ordinary-review opportunity. The State offers a different explanation involving the earlier appeal's case number; the lower court should resolve that dispute if material to its decision. The separate post-Artuz interval must also be assessed in the circumstances of an incarcerated person acting without counsel. Eight or nine months does not itself establish a failure of reasonable pursuit.

The dissent disagrees that assuming Gonzalez's account makes the proper discretionary assessment unnecessary. In its view, that assumption cannot replace consideration below of the whole combination after removal of the categorical bar. The remand permits denial if the complete showing remains insufficient; it gives Gonzalez an adjudication under Rule 60 rather than an entitlement to reopening, a certificate or a writ. These remedial and application positions are noncontrolling.

### 2005-06-23 — [Green Party of New York State v. New York State Board of Elections; 389 F.3d 411 (Second Circuit).](../records/green-party-v-new-york-state-board-of-elections_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.** The Chief Justice regards the demonstrated loss of affiliation records, organizing opportunities and equal access as severe. Burdick's severe-burden rule therefore requires a narrowly drawn restriction serving a compelling interest. The organizations' continuing ability to campaign does not adequately measure the injury to this established channel of political identity and organization. Erasure does not administer the independent primary threshold, and clear instructions address confusion without destroying the association. He would sustain the injunction under that stricter standard.

He joins the Court's interim-protection, functional-implementation and common-policy remedy holdings. To make that relief effective, he would require concrete form, record and publication compliance dates tied to the next registration cycle, immediate preservation of existing data, and reports showing correction. Any alternative administration must provide equivalent protection, and any narrower relief must fully cure the common injury for the objectively qualified category.

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by Justice Thomas.** The State may define official enrollment as part of an administrable primary system. The dissent accepts that political affiliation is protected and that the lists have real organizing value. It nonetheless regards the added benefits of a publicly financed system as an ordinary burden rather than the substantial restriction the Court finds. The organizations remain able to nominate, campaign and associate, and the State may link its enrollment service to the parties whose primaries it administers.

Burdick permits reasonable regulatory administration and does not require the State to await actual confusion or disruption. The dissent acknowledges that these organizations have demonstrated statewide support and that the challenged erasure removes useful information. Those facts do not, in its view, require continuing state recognition and data services beyond the defined primary structure. It would reverse the interlocutory judgment and dissolve the injunction.

### 2005-06-23 — [Halbert v. Michigan, No. 03-10198.](../records/halbert-v-michigan_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Justice Thomas, dissenting, joined by Justice Scalia.** The dissent would sustain the judgment on two independently sufficient grounds. First, Douglas governs an appeal as of right, while Ross permits a State to limit counsel at a genuinely selective stage. Michigan affords a leave application after a plea has narrowed the possible issues and supplies appointment in defined circumstances, including after leave is granted. The absence of an earlier counseled appellate presentation is significant, but the dissent does not regard it as sufficient to extend Douglas to this different procedure. Robbins governs the required assistance once that entitlement exists; it does not itself settle this classification.

Second, assuming an entitlement, the dissent finds an actual waiver from the reported record. Halbert repeatedly confirmed the changed appellate rights and finite appointment categories. Counsel's compliance response and the trial court's understanding-and-voluntariness finding reinforce the inference that he understood and accepted the ordinary absence of appointed assistance. The statutory duty supplies the legal context, rather than proof of unreported advice.

The dissent recognizes that surrender of an appeal as of right differs from surrender of help preparing an available application. It nevertheless reads the cumulative advice and confirmations as sufficiently conveying that residual consequence. Under Tovar and Stumpf, demonstrated understanding may be inferred without an express residual exclusion or a prescribed waiver formula. The subsequent requests do not negate the knowing choice the dissent finds. It would affirm on either ground.

### 2005-06-23 — [Kelo v. City of New London, No. 04-108.](../records/kelo-v-city-of-new-london_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Justice Kennedy, concurring.** Kennedy joins the Court's opinion in full. Ordinary deference must still examine a supported allegation that private favoritism supplies the actual object of a taking. The rational-basis inquiries in Cleburne and Moreno illustrate that judicial respect for governmental choices can coexist with a genuine examination of an impermissible object. The plan, decision process and beneficiary relationships matter to that inquiry; merely identifying incidental private gain does not establish invalidity.

The record supports this comprehensive plan under that standard. Kennedy leaves open whether a narrowly drawn, exceptionally suspicious class of transfers could warrant stronger review. He identifies no governing heightened standard or set of triggers for that different category in this case.

**Justice O'Connor, dissenting, joined by Justices Scalia and Thomas.** The dissent would prohibit taking ordinary private property for new private users solely because their normal activities are expected to produce greater economic benefits. Public ownership and actual public service remain valid. Berman addressed a harmful area, and Midkiff a harmful concentration of ownership; the acquisition itself removed the identified condition. The petitioners' ordinary homes present a materially different case.

The precedents' broad public-purpose language favors the City's position, but the dissent would confine that language at this economic-successor boundary. A sincere comprehensive plan does not cure the substantive problem because virtually every private activity yields incidental public gains. Meaningful pretext review addresses bad purpose but leaves the lawful-purpose rule too broad. Compensation and possible state-law protection cannot replace an independent federal public-use limit. The dissent would reverse and remand for relief preventing these condemnations.

**Justice Thomas, dissenting.** Thomas also joins the common dissent as an alternative under existing precedents, while proposing a further change to their constitutional rationale. The public-use condition should require governmental ownership or use, or a legally enforceable entitlement of the public to use the property. Common-carrier property may satisfy that rule despite private title; projected jobs and taxes from ordinary private premises do not.

The constitutional text and structure distinguish public use from the independent duty to compensate. Cole and Kohl illustrate public functions and the private-use prohibition without proving an exhaustive historical catalogue. Thomas would reconsider Berman's and Midkiff's broader public-purpose propositions and incompatible private-transfer permission. He would replace Boston & Maine's broader rationale while recognizing that secured passenger service could satisfy the narrower use rule. His alternative reliance on the common dissent does not amount to approval of those precedents' permanent constitutional correctness.

### 2005-06-23 — [Mayle v. Felix, No. 04-563.](../records/mayle-v-felix_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

**Justice Stevens, dissenting, joined by Justice Souter.** The dissent treats the trial's use of the challenged evidence as the relevant connected occurrence. Rule 15(c) deliberately permits some amendments despite expiration of limitations; its text does not itself require every habeas ground to be a separate transaction. The State received a timely challenge to the trial's validity, and both the videotape and interrogation theories concern evidence introduced there.

The dissent acknowledges the distinct interrogation facts and does not claim that Felix originally pleaded coercion. It instead reads the transaction broadly in this pending attack on one conviction. Section 2242 provides ordinary civil amendment, and Rule 15(a) allows courts to reject abuse, delay or prejudice. The timely original filing and the different regime governing later actions after final judgment provide additional limits. Those safeguards, in its view, answer the Court's finality concern without narrowing occurrence to each constitutional episode.

That reading also preserves the usefulness of counsel appointed after a prisoner's initial filing: later identification of a trial defect need not be excluded simply because an unrepresented petition used a different legal or factual route. The dissent would affirm the relation-back ruling and permit the remaining proceedings under the ordinary amendment rules.

### 2005-06-23 — [Orff v. United States, No. 03-1566.](../records/orff-v-united-states_merits_2005-06-23.md#public-projection)

The unanimous common ground establishes no separate-writing function.

### 2005-06-27 — [Bell v. Thompson, No. 04-514.](../records/bell-v-thompson_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Justice Stevens, joined by Justices Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, dissenting.** The dissent would affirm the limited correction and hearing direction. Rule 41(b)'s enlargement authority and the absence of an issued mandate leave room to complete a materially deficient first review. The Sultan report and deposition supplied a concrete evidentiary problem bearing on the existing mitigation claim, and the appellate panel's examination of that problem supports its decision to reopen the record before its disposition became effective.

Carlisle requires an authorized procedural basis, while the institutional-process position in Calderon distinguishes correction from reopening completed merits merely to reach a preferred answer. Here the mandate never issued, and the revised judgment directs further adjudication rather than awards the writ. The delay and failure to notify deserve serious criticism, but the dissent does not regard them as sufficient to make this bounded correction unreasonable. Review for abuse of discretion should give weight to the panel's detailed examination of the omitted evidence instead of replacing its judgment about the need for a hearing. The hearing remains subject to independently applicable habeas law. The dissent rests on the documented omission and actual procedural stage, without assuming clerical fault, diligence, innocence or ultimately prejudicial ineffective assistance.

### 2005-06-27 — [McCreary County v. American Civil Liberties Union of Kentucky, No. 03-1693.](../records/mccreary-county-v-aclu-of-kentucky_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** The Chief Justice joins the Court's opinion and judgment. Because the challenged conduct is a common governmental display, he would require the counties to substantiate that any narrower cure fully protects the persons exposed to it. The counties must maintain compliance with the interim restraint and submit any replacement they seek to use in a form permitting judicial review of its actual content, setting and official authorization. The district court must reject cosmetic compliance, permit an effective lawful cure and proceed toward final adjudication. The repeal receives real weight in that assessment; it neither automatically establishes a cure nor permanently disables the counties from lawful historical presentation. These additional substantiation and submission requirements are his separate remedial position.

**Justice Scalia, joined by Justices Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent applies the same governmental-function inquiry but finds the third arrangement sufficiently changed to serve historical explanation. The original displays, religious resolutions and selective additions are relevant evidence. Their force must nevertheless be weighed against the later presentation of complete documents in equal frames and the actual explanation of their place in legal history, with the translation label and scriptural citation already removed. In the dissent's assessment, these changes alter the official act itself rather than merely place a secular title over the earlier presentation.

Agostini permits government to explain religious influences on law while prohibiting official adoption of doctrine. Robinson distinguishes historical exposition from an authoritative civil religious identifier. Marshfield directs attention to what government actually presents; its private-ownership conclusion is inapplicable to these county exhibits, but its functional inquiry still requires credit for a real change. The later repeal removes the resolutions' formal force and reinforces the already sufficient transformation. The dissent would reverse and dissolve the preliminary injunction against the third arrangements on that ground. Kennedy and Thomas join this complete historical-function application, including consideration of the earlier official acts.

### 2005-06-27 — [Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer Studios Inc. v. Grokster, Ltd., No. 04-480.](../records/metro-goldwyn-mayer-studios-v-grokster_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Justice Ginsburg, concurring, joined by Justice Kennedy.** The concurrence joins the inducement opinion and would also reject the distributors' summary judgment under Sony. The relevant inquiry concerns these products' reasonable capability for commercially significant lawful use, rather than benefits attributed to peer-to-peer technology in general. Particularized evidence matters even though lawful use need not be predominant and genuine future markets count.

The declarations and anecdotal examples do not, in this view, establish that capability beyond genuine dispute. General statements about authorized distribution, public-domain works or possible future uses may describe real opportunities without proving the commercially significant prospect asserted for these products. The available-file survey does not establish actual-download proportions; its residual is unknown, and the owners continued to dispute the lawful-use showing. A legal assumption that all uncertainty favors sufficient capability would give too little force to Sony's substantive condition.

The concurrence therefore sees an additional reason to set aside the distributors' judgment and would revisit this theory on a fuller record if inducement does not dispose of the case. It would not grant the owners' motion automatically, adopt a numerical requirement, or make infringement percentages, economic dependence or nonfiltering an independent final-liability test.

**Justice Breyer, concurring, joined by Justices Stevens and O'Connor.** This concurrence joins vacatur on inducement while accepting the lower court's favorable Sony capability determination. Concrete authorized and public-domain uses, together with their plausible development, give practical force to the difference between present misuse and the product's lawful capabilities. The survey's unknown residual is not a proved lawful percentage, and the position requires no precise numerical finding from it.

Sony protects technological development when a tool can serve substantial lawful purposes even though current users predominantly infringe. Requiring detailed profitability plans for each future use, or replacing capability with an inquiry into the distributor's present dependence on infringement, would make that protection uncertain and expose useful technologies before lawful markets have developed. The government's business-centered alternative would shift this balance without a sufficiently demonstrated need or administrable advantage.

The concurrence evaluates the proposed change against Sony's protection of innovation, the strength of the demonstrated enforcement need and the comparative costs of alteration. That assessment is a reason to retain Sony, not a new three-step liability test. Inducement and direct-infringement enforcement provide protection for copyright; Napster's distinct knowing-assistance route remains available. Its protective account concerns liability inferred from distribution alone and does not immunize purposeful promotion or proved material assistance. It would preserve the Sony determination while returning the independent inducement claim for adjudication.

### 2005-06-27 — [National Cable & Telecommunications Association v. Brand X Internet Services, No. 04-277; FCC v. Brand X Internet Services, No. 04-281.](../records/national-cable-telecommunications-association-v-brand-x-internet-services_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Justice Breyer, concurring.** Breyer joins the Court's opinion in full. Congress's assignment, not expertise or complexity, supplies an agency's interpretive authority; formal procedure is neither necessary nor sufficient to establish it, and the particular question must be one Congress left to the agency. The definitions turn on what the provider actually offers. This classification lies close to the boundary, but subscribers are offered transmission of their chosen information for a fee, and the Commission's account of integration does not move that function outside the definition. Within the statutory category the Commission keeps broad discretion: it decides which duties apply and how they operate, makes the technical and market findings section 160 requires, and must forbear when it makes them. Part II respects actual delegation because an earlier decision that left a choice open does not constrain it. The opinion adopts no exception based on describing a question as important and no rule that an agency loses authority whenever a court speaks first.

**Justice Scalia, concurring in part.** Scalia joins Parts II, III and IV and the judgment, but not Part I. As his Mead dissent explained, he would give controlling reasonable-choice treatment to an agency's authoritative official interpretation within an assigned statutory field rather than ask whether Congress authorized the particular mechanism to carry the force of law. That difference does not affect this case: the Commission's nationwide declaratory ruling is its authoritative position on any view, and the statutory definitions, which bind an agency even within an assigned field, exclude the classification. He adds that executive displacement of a judicially fixed statutory meaning would raise the judicial-finality concern illustrated by Chicago & Southern Air Lines v. Waterman: a completed construction by an Article III court may not be overturned by the Executive, and its force rests on judicial power as well as on precedent. Neal's particular holding does not itself dictate a universal prohibition, and he joins Part II's bounded rule; the finality ground is his alone.

**Justice Thomas, dissenting, joined by Justices Stevens, O'Connor and Kennedy.** The dissenters join Part I and would reverse in both dockets. An earlier court of appeals' selection of a preferred statutory reading does not, in their view, foreclose a different reasonable agency interpretation within a choice Congress actually delegated when the earlier holding and the statute leave that choice open. A binding determination that the statute requires a particular answer remains a legal boundary, and which kind of holding exists depends on its substance rather than on an express ambiguity formula. Portland construed the transmission component without deciding the offering question as one with a single lawful answer, so it did not foreclose the Commission's construction; placing the burden on the agency gives the first circuit to rule a choice Congress assigned to the Commission. The dissent's position concerns circuit precedent; the different force of this Court's constructions, which may settle statutory meaning and remove ambiguity, is not at issue.

On classification, the statute contemplates information service provided via telecommunications without making every transmission input a separate public offering. The Commission's paragraphs 38–41 describe genuine retrieval, storage, email and access functions integrated with cable transmission and going beyond internal network management; on that account the finished offering is an information service within the statutory range. A telephone service packaged with voicemail would keep its separate identity, but this offering is functionally integrated, and Gulf Power left the question open. The dissenters would also reject the cable/DSL arbitrariness objection: the Commission's paragraphs 42–44 rely on the Computer II framework for telephone-company facilities, those carriers' bottleneck history, emerging broadband alternatives and investment objectives, and Smiley permits that explained distinction within lawful authority. They would sustain the classification as permissible, not compelled, and adopt no general bundling exemption.

### 2005-06-27 — [Town of Castle Rock v. Gonzales, No. 04-278.](../records/town-of-castle-rock-v-gonzales_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** The Chief Justice joins the Court's opinion in full. He would further specify that a beneficiary plausibly denied the secured protective service by municipal policy states a procedural claim when officials fail to provide a practicable, timely opportunity to establish the statutory conditions and receive an intelligible determination. Receiving the order and material information, genuinely assessing the conditions and communicating a reason for refusal can allow a mistaken refusal to be reconsidered in time without delaying emergency action through a formal hearing. Monell still requires proof that the Town's policy caused the procedural deprivation. Any compensatory recovery must correspond to proved causal injury; the children's deaths cannot automatically measure damages merely because an enforcement duty existed.

**Justice Stevens, joined by Justice Ginsburg, concurring.** The concurrence joins the Court's opinion in full and adds that property interests protected by due process are created and defined by state law. A State that deliberately replaces general nonenforcement discretion with a specific protective duty owed to named beneficiaries creates the kind of entitlement Roth protects. Enforcement is itself the tangible service, so a beneficiary who insists on it does not claim a right to procedure alone, and refusal of the service differs from an incorrect assessment of its conditions.

The Colorado courts have the final word on the meaning of Colorado law. When a dispositive state-law question is genuinely unresolved, federal courts should obtain or await the State's authoritative construction rather than supply one of their own. No such question is dispositive here: the statute's commands and the order supply the entitlement, and a later compilation label creates no uncertainty requiring certification.

**Justice Souter, concurring.** Justice Souter joins the Court's opinion in full and writes to explain why the claim does not seek process to obtain process. A procedural due-process claim cannot rest on an interest consisting only of the procedure itself, and Doe v. District of Columbia's investigative-procedure holding rests on that principle. The benefit here is different. Colorado secures to the protected person the protective assistance itself, enforcement of the order when its conditions exist, much as state law may secure utility service, public education or benefits. The procedures by which officers determine whether those conditions exist are distinct from that assistance, and the federal process claimed concerns those determinations. Because the Court's opinion identifies the protected object in that way, preserves the exclusion of general investigative duties and promises no outcome, its rule does not convert enforcement procedure into property.

**Justice Scalia, joined by Justices O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent would reverse and restore dismissal of the reviewed municipal procedural-due-process theory. In its view, a state direction that police take specified enforcement steps upon qualifying violations does not create a beneficiary's constitutional property interest without an independently secured substantive benefit distinct from that enforcement procedure. Colorado's commands have legal force, but their object is the executive enforcement mechanism against another person. An identified beneficiary and defined triggers do not transform the prescribed steps into a separately secured benefit, and the reverse-side notice does not make the police parties to a decree. Doe v. District of Columbia distinguishes such an entitlement from mandatory investigation or enforcement procedures, and Sandin's attention to substantive constraints supplies no rule for property in police action. The order's parenting permissions also show that the claimed interest cannot be an unconditional right to the children's return.

The dissent assumes that the statutory duties are mandatory and does not rest on unlimited police discretion or on whether the interest can be sold or assigned a monetary value. It concludes that a binding direction can regulate officials without creating constitutional property in the process they must follow, so the pleaded policy cannot supply the missing interest, and it does not reach the adequacy of the process provided.

### 2005-06-27 — [Van Orden v. Perry, No. 03-1500.](../records/van-orden-v-perry_merits_2005-06-27.md#public-projection)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** The Chief Justice joins the Court's rule, application and effective-cure mandate. He would additionally require Texas to substantiate that any narrower adjustment fully cures the established function. He would require the district court, after receiving a proposed cure, to set a concrete compliance schedule, require evidence of the completed arrangement, retain enforcement authority and enjoin continuing unlawful presentation if Texas fails to comply. A nominal disclaimer alone cannot substitute for a change in what the State actually presents.

**Justice Breyer, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent applies the same prohibition on official adoption of religious doctrine but finds that Texas's complete presentation performs historical commemoration. The donor attribution, ordinary treatment of donated monuments, visitor materials, civic collection and preservation history explain how the religious artifact is used. The commands retain religious meaning, yet that meaning is presented as part of the history commemorated on the grounds rather than as a State demand for religious assent.

Agostini makes that actual function decisive. Robinson permits historical exposition while forbidding a faith's use as a permanent civil badge, and Marshfield requires examination of what the public is actually shown. State ownership distinguishes this monument from private speech, but does not make historical preservation itself an adoption of the artifact's beliefs. The monument's age helps explain its preserved commemorative role; it is not an exemption from review. The dissent would affirm on the full contextual account, without a separate rule allowing religious acknowledgment, favoring monotheism or requiring coercion before governmental adoption can be prohibited.

## Source and continuation limits

Opening law remains synchronized through June 30, 2004. Each Record states its event-specific cutoff, verified source map and actual publication scope. Current law now includes the completed events through June 27, 2005, preserving the pre-date baseline for each same-date group. Brown uses November 23, 1992 as its distinct substantive-law cutoff; conduct-date immunity remains tied to the conduct at issue. Shepard's complete charging, waiver and forfeiture instructions govern its remand. Conduct-date notice and Bell’s state-adjudication cutoff remain distinct as stated in the earlier Records. Section 703 remains the only admitted noncase-law source. No later filing, report, remedial completion or new original-action event is presumed. The source-control notes distinguish authenticated published recitations and supported bounded premises from unrecovered originals; no missing research source establishes litigant default. Chunk 4 validation preserves the fresh-context source checks and corrected pre-adjudication finality intake. Only published positions from the completed Records enter this projection. The generated Render Input copies each bounded Public Projection; public Render is a separate task.

Chunk 8 applies the entering law effective before May 23, May 31 and June 6, 2005 respectively. Its later-date screens use only completed earlier-date Public Projections. Published lower-court recitations and the bounded source limits stated in each Record support adjudication; missing historical source material creates no litigant default. All eleven scheduled chunk 8 events are terminal at the reviewed stage, with lower-court remands or further proceedings only as their mandates provide. Medellín dismisses the pending writ while leaving the independently pending Texas proceeding to its own course.

The June 6, June 13 and June 20 decisions become entering law only for later dates. Their linked Public Projections supply the complete authority, qualifications and remedies. Alaska remains pending for its conforming decree. The remaining scheduled inventory proceeds from the manifest; no undecided same-date event is treated as authority.

Chunk 10 uses separate entering-law cutoffs before June 20, June 23 and June 27, 2005. Completed earlier-date Public Projections were copied to fresh continuation stages; no same-day companion supplied entering law. The generated chunk handoff copies each completed Record. Public rendering remains a separate task.
